===== 0002601_19050812_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The British Gold Plate.—The gold plate possessed by His Majesty the King, is only equalled by that of the Russian Crown. and when this treasure appears at a banquet in St. George's Hall, Windsor Castle, or at Buckingham Palace, as it has done on several occasions during the past season, the effect is dazzling and gorgeous in the extreme. Only' a few of our noble familes possess very much gold plate,but that owned by the Duke of Bedford is magnificent. Among the owners of valuable silver plate may be mentioned the Duke of Buccleuch, Lord Lousdowne, and Lord Wolseley. The latter possess some wonderful old Irish silver gathered together during his command in Dublin. A Popular Host and Hostess.—Great regret has been felt for Mr. Willie Javes' illness, and it was the more unfortunate that this should have coincided with Good- wood, and so interfered with the house parties at West Dean Park. which as usual had been arranged for the races. Both Mr. and Mrs. Willie James are great favourites with the King and Queen, and their Majesties have, upon several occasions honoured them with their presence at West Dean Park. Mrs. James is one of the cleverest and wittiest women in Society, and is always exquisitely dressed. The Czarina.—Siuce the birth of the little Tzarevitch, the Czarina has lost all her habitual reserve and taci- turnity and there is little doubt that now this barrier between the young Empress and her subjects is with- drawn, she will become a great favourite. The Czarina is a beautiful woman, but up to a few months ago, a cold, hard expression did much towards marring her beauty. In the matter of dress, the Empress of all the Russia's is utterly indifferent, and her happiest hours are spent in her nurseries, which she personally superintends. Madame de Navarro.—Many hundreds of congratula- tory messages reached Madame de Navarro (Miss Mary Anderson) so soon as the news of the birth of a little daughter, to this once more popular actress of her day, became known. Madame de Navarro has one son, Jose Maria, aho is now nine years of age, and it is just six- teen years since she married sad left the stage. Madame de Navarro is considered to be even a more beautiful woman than she was a girl, which is saying much. as up to the time of her retirement, she was acclaimed quite the most beautiful actress on the stage. Fashionable Scent.—When perfume is used at all, the natural perfume of a flower,not merely an essence called by its name, is chosen by the fashionable womaa. The sweet pea blossom scent is having a great vogue just now, so also is the fragrance of the night-scented stock. Sachets worn in the different items of the toilette, and -placed in wardrobes and all receptacles for clothes' are however to be preferred to scent in liquid form. It is surprising bow many Royal ladies there are, who have chosen and remained faithful to the perfume of the violet, a scent beloved by our own Queen, and the Empress of Russia. Sun Headaches.—A sensible white linen sunshade lined with dark blue or dark green, should always be added to the holiday outfit, of the girl or woman who suffers 1 with headache brought on by the glare of the sun. The usual unlined parasol, or ea butcas is of no use when a powerful sun is in qu.stion, and the sunshade described is a great improvement upon ugly green spectacles. A I hat with a shady brim should also be worn by the sufferer from sua headache, moreover she will exper- ience further relief from a dark blue, or dark green gauze veil. A Velvet Season.—Velvet promises to be greatly worn during the coming autumn, entire gowns and wraps being built of it, while the majority of cloth, tweed, homespun and serge costumes in course of re- paration show collars, cuffs, and other forms of trim- ming of this particular fabric. The velvet buttons which appear upon all toilettes so ornamented are of enormous size. or else they run to the other extreme and very pettily reach vanishing point. Milliners are again lining, or partially lining, the brims of many of their newest hats with velvet, and this is a mode so eminently becoming that it is likely to. be greatly appreciated. About Gloves.—Much of the afterwear and good fit of a glove, depends upon the careful manner in which it is first put on. A spare half hour should be utilised for the purpose, when the hands ase cool, the fingers being worked on quite straight. A little glove powder should be dusted into the gloves before they are worn, and the buttons firmly sewn on. When removing gloves it is wiser to slip them over the hand, inside out, then they should be turned, pulled out, and laid to air for an hour or two. .

Packing Cut Flowers.—The recipients of boxes of cut flowers have often considerable cause to lament the want of a little care and forethought on the part of the packers. In the first place the flowers should be gathered in the early morning, before the sun is on them, next they should be placed in water for at feast two hours, the whole of the stalk but none of the blossom being submerged. Further, an hour befoto packing, the stems should be placed in tepid water, aria if a little moist cotton-wool is twined round them as the blooms are carefully stowed away, in the usual cardboard receptacle, there need be little fear that the flowers will arrive at their destination looking faded and crushed. The Care of Irons.—The housewife who neglects to look after her irons, is in much the same position as the man who refrained from waiting to sharpen his 4W. After use, irons should always be polished, and if the least suspicion of starch still adheres to them, they should be rubbed upon a board, on which has teem sprinkled a little powdered bathbrick. When rust or roughness attacks irons, they must be stood in a bath of paraffin for 24 hours, then they should be rubbed with a paste of bathbrick and paraffin, end finally polished with dry bathbrick. Trons should always be kept in a dry place and never placed face downwards. Home Neglect.—The modern housewife is the subject of severe censure in an article written by Mrs. Hugh Jackson, in one of the current magazines. She was, according to the writer, the best housekeeper in the world, and she is now the worst. This sweeping con- demnation seems to be in accordance with the idea, which is not peculiar to any one generation, that men, women, manners, and customs, are not so good as they used to he. We hear a great deal about the decadence of home life, but our mothers and grandmothers used to say much the same thing as is being said now, and declare that things were very different to what they were in their early days. If home life is decaying, it has been decaying a good many hundreds of years, but at tho same time, no one would deny that there have been marked changes in recent years in our home life. The further employment of women in all the pursuits of life is one contributing cause, and the greater love of riches and of show and display is another. It is however quite wrong to suppose that it is only women who neglect their homes in the pursuit of pleasure. The fault as often as not, does not rest with them at all, but with husbands who take a one-sided view of this question.

ST. HELENS.

SCHOLARS' FESTIVITIF,S AT ST. HELEN.—August Monday was very largely devoted in St. Helens to the pleasure of the children attending the various Sunday schools. During the morning numerous brass bands made their way to various parts of the town where the sr:loot children were in waiting. The big musters were at Lowe House, Holy Cross, and Sacred Heart Roman Catholic schools. Sacred Heart School, numbering about 1,600 children, went to linowsley-road to a field lent by Mr. Huhn°, where the usual sports and games were indulged in, and tea was served. At Denton's Green there was a very large attendance at the field where Holy Cross (1,300) and Lowe House (IWO) joined. The annual sports of the men's guilds were contested with great spirit, St. Mark's Church Sunday Schools and Bible class were the guests of Alderman C. Gamble and Mrs. Gamble at Haresflnch Park, and spent a most enjoyable afternoon. The Congregational Schools, Brook-street, were joined by Newtown and Gernird's Bridge, and enjoyed themselves at a field at Windle Smithies. The Wesleyan schools went to Moss Bank, and the Primitive Methodists to Knowsley-road. Apart from these outings the day passed off very quietly

A BATSMAN'S DEATH.

At Glossop on 1110-n-d—ai-WCO-ro-rner'S. inquiry was held regarding the death of Isaac Berry, who was killed whilst batting for the Hurst cticket team against Glossop on Saturday. The ball hit him on the chest in the region of the heart,and his death occurred immedi- atley. A doctor who gave evidence said that death was caused by the blow, and the soundest heart might be stopped by a similar shock. The ground was in good condition and no blame was attached to the bowler. The verdict of the jury was that death was due to accident.

A NOVEL FRUIT-BASKET.

Fruit arranged amongst its own leaves in a basket can be made into an artistic centre-piece for a luncheon or dinner table by the employ- ment of a little ingenuity. An ordinary 31b. wicker basket—such as is used for packing strawberries—will answer the purpose. Give it a coating of gold, silver, or lustre paint, which can be obtained ready for use at any oil-shop; twist a bright ribbon round the handle and tie in a bow at the top, and when you have filled it with apples, pears, grapes or plums, and given them a lining of green leaves, you will be sur- prised how pretty and effective is this simple table decoration. The Aznall strawberry baskets may be treated in the same fashion, but as they are unprovided with handles, it will be necessary to improvise one out of a bent cane or a piece of wire, which is concealed from view by the twisted ribbon. A "ST" GOWN.

As the summer is becoming somewhat ad- vanced, the woman who finds •herself in need of a "best" gown will be wise to turn her attention to voile and similar materials in preference to the filmy muslins and lawns which have hitherto occupied her attention. Our sketch illustrates a gown specially designed for achievement in thin voile. In colour a delicate grey, the dress is made over a foundation of amethyst silk, which has the most delightful effect seen through the filmy upper material. The draped bodice has a

round collar, terminating. in short stole ends, of Irish crochet, and it is edged with a knife- pleating of grey silk. Short elbow sleeves are finished with large rosettes and frills of the pleated silk. The very full skirt is long all round, and has for sole trimming a shaped band of the Irish crochet above three frills of pleated silk. The suggestion of amethyst in the lining is further carried out by waistband and buttons Df the same enchanting colour; while a final touch is given by the necklace of big amethyst. coloured beads worn On the little crochet vest. THE WAY TO CLOTHE • CHILD.

A correspondent of the Lancet gives the following advice as to the best and healthiest way of clothing a girl of between fourteen and fifteen: "A thin all-wool garment, extending from the neck to just below the knees, to be worn next to the skin; this should be very thin, with short sleeves during the summer and medium weight and long sleeves during the, winter. A ' maid's ' Corset, with shoulder-straps made of stout jean, and slightly shaped; this buttons down the front, lacing behind, and hag no whalebone or steel.. Dark-blue drill knickers should be worn which button to the lower edge of the corset. The last garment is petticoat of striped Oxford shirting with calico bodice, which in the winter may be made of Scotch wineey and thin flannel respectively. These clothes should be all changed and washed weekly. It may be noticed that there is no weight on the hips or about the waist, every- thing hanging from the shoulders. Thin cash- mere stockings, reaching well above the knee, and thicker ones for the winter, should be kept up by elastic suspenders from the corset."

FOR THE SEASIDE.

A costume for seaside wear is shewn in the sketch. It is built of cream serge, and trimmed with a coarse silk braid woven in pale blue and white. The little coat is drawn in at the waist by small tucks, which give a slight fulness to the short ba:•que. Over the -Goat a rir-++T belt of pato blue kid is worn. A smart little French sailor hat worn with tins costume, simply trimmed with a pale blue ribbon and a white-spotted chiffon veil. THE BRIDGE CRAZE.

The worst part of the bridge craze is its effects on young girls, says Lady Colin Campbell. What chance is there of aagirl ever developing a love of home, when she as once drunk deep of the excitement of afternoons and evenings devoted to bridge, and the winning or losing of money? The feminine brain is not, as a rule, remarkable for its strength of balante; and its tendency to exaggeration, either in good or evil, is a well- known characteristic. It has not the elastic ,re- cuperativeness of a man's brain, which can spring back to a normal condition as soon as the momentary strain of- excitement is over; and once the love of gambling takes possession of a woman she never returns to a normal condition. Seine years ago gambling on the Stock Ex- change was the fashionable feminine pastime; but that, though it claimed a good many victims financially, had nothing like 'so disintegrating an effect on the home-life of women as bridge has succeeded in establishing. TO-DAYS RZCIPE.

----- - A VEGETABLE Sone.—Two pints whole lentils. two onions, two carrots, three sticks celery, herbs, salt and pepper, two quarts stock or water. Wash the lentils well and soak them in water for twelve hours; put them on to boil with the vegetables in weak stock. add season- ing, bon until tender; it will, take two or three hours; remove the bunch of nerbs and pulp the rost through a sieve. Return to the stew-pan. boil up, keeping it well stirred; if too thick add a little more stock or milk. Serve with fried bread.

THE BLACK MOTOR CAR

Br J. B. HARRIS BURLANA

I CHAPTER XXX. VENGENCE IS MINE!" • I ONLY ask two things of you," said , lordison, " and then you shall both go free." He was standing by the iron gate at the end of the tunnel. Arthur Holme and Lady Agnes Cliffe both faced him. Their wrists were still bound, but their faces glowed with the light of a great joy. They had been very near to death, but now life, and all that life meant to them, was within their grasp. Suddenly, and for no 'apparent reason, the man who had held them in his cruel grip, had bid them both go free. The n ght was over and the day was at hand. The sun itself, now rising from the east and turning the wide expanse of wet sand into a glory of gold. was not so resplendent as the light of freedom that had suddenly flashed up from the dark horizon of pain and death. "I only ask two things of you," repeeted Jordison, "but you must swear ein the holy cross that you will do them. In the first place, you must both swear never to reveal either directly or indirectly, this hiding-place of mine, nor give any assistance, either direct or indirect, to those who desire to capture me." "I will swear that," replied Holme, stern- ly, "though God knows ,what evil I shall dc the community." Lady Agnes murmured some words to the same -effect, and Jordison produced a small bone cross. "This cross," he said, was found in these caverns, in the bony fingers of a man whc perhaps died for his faith in the time of the Romans. It is, without doubt, a most holy and sacred relic. You will each swear the oaths I desire by your God and the hope of your future salvation, and you will each kiss this symbol of your faith to seal the words you nave spoken." Holme swore the desired oath and kissed the little cross. Then Lady Agnes did the same, repeating the words in a faint and faltering voice and shuddering as her lips touched the bone symbol of early Christianity. "Now," continued Jordison, "you must each swear to me a separate oath. You. Arthur Holme, must swear that you will tell Lady Agnes Cliffe the true story of your life, and that you will marry her before a year has passed. You, Lady Agnes Cliffe, must swear that you will marry the man you love in spite of all opposition on the part of your parents. There is nothing disgraceful in his past his- tory. He is a true and sterling man. I have done him a great wrong, and I intend „to repair it. Do you both agree to swear these oaths ? " The two you •; people looke I at each other in 'bewilderment. They mild hardly bel eve heir ears. Jordison had attempted both 'heir lives, and now as an alternative to tie:ie., he asked them to swear to fulfil the dearest wish of their hearts. They could understanc the necessity of the first oath, but the second one was incomprehensible "Why," began Bolme in a stammering voice. "Don't ask any questions," broke in Jordi- son savagely and abruptly, "for I shan't answer them. Will you swear ? " "I will, so help' me God," Holme said fer- vently "if Lady Agnes—" and he stopped and • looked enquiringly at the woman be loved. "Yes, Arthur," she said faintly. Her white face flushed with crimSon, and there was a tender light in her eyes. They both swore the oath, and once more they kissed the crosl. "The hardway goes round Po the back of the island," said jordison. " Keep dose to the land till you come to a white piece of rock. Then strike across the sand in a straiOt line between the white rock and a black post on the shore beyond the salt marsh. You will than find a read and have no difficulty in reaching home. Be sure and keep the strai:zlit line between the points I have named. On either side of you lies an enemy that -wilt not be so merciful to you as William Jordison." With these words Jordison took out a knife and cut their bonds and they were free. Without another word he unlocked the iron gate, and swinging it back on its hinges, stood aside to let them pass. They went out together without a word. Jordison watched their figures disappear round the end of the tunnel. He closed the gate with a crash and locked it. Then he turned abruptly on his heel and picking up a lamp from the ground, ' retraced his steps into the tunnel. And so ; William Jordison went back into the dark- ness, and his son went out into the light. j Twelve hours later the black car stood in the centre of the cavern ready for its last journey. Jordison had decided to run it to within a few miles of Liverpool before day- break, and to ritake his way from that port to America. He had shaved ofr his beard and moustache, and his gaunt face looked wolfish and hideous in its nakedness. I.ipp had packed such things as they re- quired for the voyage in two trunks, and I placed them on the car. Concealed about their clothes, sewn here and there iu linings, scattered singly so as to escape the notice of the most diligent Customs' officer, were 450,000 worth of jewels. They had been picked from their settings and occupied an amount of space that was ridiculously small eompared to their value. Lipp lit the lamps, and the two men took th6ir seats in the car. Then Jordison started the engines, and a few seconds later they glided through the open door and slipped down the long narrow passage to the sea. When they emerged into the open the moon was shining brightly over the waste of sand and water. Jordison steered slowly round the island till he reached the white rock, then he turned off at right angles and made straight for a twinkling speck of yellow in the west. The black post was invisible at night, but this light, which shone from the West Ray Light- ship, lay some ten miles beyond in the same straight line. They crossed the dangerous sands in safety, though every now and then a sudden sinking of the wheel warned the driver that he had run over the edge of the track. Then they glided up a gentle slope, passed over the edge of the bank which kept the tide out of the low lying land, and went slowly westwards over the marshes. The road was execrable. It was little more than a grass track, and the stones that had originally formed its foundation :protruded through the thin layer of soil. It ran parallel 'to the road leading to the Red House, some eight miles north of it, and joined the old Roman Causeway. It had been originally constructed to bring up sand and shingle from the beach, but had not been used for many years. Where it joined the Causeway it had sunk several inches, and was submerged for a hundred yards in a shallow lake of water. On either side of it lay many miles of desolate marsh lands When the motor reached the main road Jordison turned to the left and went south- ward. Lipp pointed out in picturesque language that this was not the way to Liver- pool. "I am aware of that, Lipp," Jordison re- plied grimly. "I have to call at Heather- stone Hall first." Lipp pointed out the risk and broke into a torrent of oaths at Jordison's fool-hardiness. But the latter intimated that his servant Gould leave the car then and there if he liked, laid Lipp relapsed into sullen silence. Shortly after II o'clock the car glided under the shadows of the overhanging trees in th park. Jordison alighted, and told Lipp to keep his eyes and ears open. If anyone dis- covered the car he was to run straight back to the cavern. He, Jordison, would understand what had happened, and could make his way, to their hiding-place on foot. heWhen he had given these final instructions, climbed over the wall and made his way through the plantation into the park. gaunt— face looked horrible in the -moonlight. The loss of his beard and moustache laid bare all the evil lines of his features. The head of a vulture, peering found for the dead carcase of man or beast was not more loathly to look upon.

Ana Jordison's errand that night was death. Re had resolved to kill Marie de la Mottle before he left England. Then, at last, his life-work would be accomplished. She had taken nalife, but she had rained that . which was more precious than mere physical existence—the soul of a man who might have . done much good, but who had sunk lower than the beasts that perish. He knew then that it was the face of the Countess of Heatherstone. He passed his hands under the edge of the mask, and suddenly gripped the throat.

But directly ' his fingers closed on the neck he whipped his hands away and staggered back from the bed, with difficulty suppressing a cry of horror. The flesh was cold as marble, and the chill of it went through his whole body from head to foot. He crept through the darkness till he found the switch by the door. Then he turned the light on and looked towards the bed. There lay the Countess of Heatherstone with her arms folded across her breast, and a cross ol white flowers at her feet. William Jordison went over and looked at her long and earnest. ly. The white hand of death had hidden the ravages of disease, as snow hides the ugli- ness of a devastated land. Then suddenly he stepped forward, and re- placing the velvet mask on her face crept from the room like a beaten hound. face, had taken her punishment into his own hands, and William Jordison knew that his Maker had discarded him, as a man discards a broke, knife.

AND

THE world's roses are estimated by Mr. J. G. Baker, F.R.S., to include 69 primary species. Of these 29 grow native in Europe; 26 in Northern Asia; 18 in Western Asia; 9 in India; 10 in Western North America; 6 in Eastern North America.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
A DENTIST'S SPRING-MOTOR.

The spring-motor of Dr. Bercut, a French surgeon-dentist, rotates a flexible cable 500 to 1,500 times per minute, running twelve minutes to an hour and a-half, according to the work performed, and is intended for dental and bone- cutting operations, massaging, &c. It is wound by the help of a metallic cord. It has proved exceedingly useful where electric power is not available, and can be taken to patients' homes, or elsewhere.

THE WEIGHT OF THE BODY IN WATER. The human body has been lately proved by Dr. Ferrier, a French investigator, to be some- times heavier and sometimes lighter than pure water, so that a person may float in salt water and sink in fresh. Abnormal lightness seems to occur in certain victims of hysteria and ner- vous disease, and in witchcraft days the " trial by water" would have convicted such unfortu- nates as being in league with the devil.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
VENOMOUS LARVA.

M. Laveran has made an interesting an- nouncement to the French Academy of Sciences, based upon the researches of M. Phisalix. This communication is to the effect that the " eggs" of bees contain the same poison prin- ciple as the venom of the sting itself. This fact, it is claimed, has been established by ab- solutely convincing experiments on birds. As the fixation of venom by the " eggs " is stated to hold good of all venomous creatures, the dis- covery has an obvious importance as throwing light on the phenomena of heredity.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW TO KEEP YOUNG.

The art of keeping young, we are told by Pro- fessor Goldschneider, of Berlin, consists in keep- ing sound the blood-vessels, for we are only as old as the heart and the arteries. Long life is best ensured by early attention to the heart, though care begun in old age will often accost plish much. A great danger lies in abnormal demands on the organ, and little ,injuries accum- ulate, so that serious harm must come from the abuse of alcohol or tobacco, of eating too much, of too great muscular effort, or even of mental overwork. Moderation in all things is required. Exercise should not be violent nor too little in amount, elasticity of the blood-vessels should be promoted by frequent exposure of the skin to the air and water, and work, food, and sleep should bo th ough t v adjoe-e,I. Old people need constant movement, with not too much sleep.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
ASTRAL REFRACTION.

The atmosphere through which we view the heavenly bodies being curved like a watch glass, causes a certain deflection in their apparent line of direction as observed from the earth, and in- troduces a certain difficulty into the study of their laws of motion. M. Locury has kid before the French Academy of Sciences a device per. fected by himself for correcting tkis deviation. Selecting two stars, near enough to be viewed through one lens, he takes an exact measure- ment by moans of two mirrors held in the hand like two branches of a compass of the angle which separates them. This angle is, of course, always the same, since the two stars always respond in parallel degree to the apparent move- ment of the celestial sphere. Any difference in the angle is, therefore, that of the atmospheric refraction.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
REVOLVING SEA-LIGHTS.

The characteristic features which differentiate the light given by one lighthouse from that given by another have been introduced for various rea.sons, says a writer in the Windsor Magazine. The substitution of revolving in place of fixed sealights arises naturally enough from the in- creasing number of bright fixed lights on ships and on shore. The group-flashing system, which is acknowledged to afford the best character- istics for revolving lights, wits brought forward by Messrs. Chance in 1874, under the superin- tendence of their then scientific adviser, the late Dr. John Hopkinson, who invented it, and it has been adopted by all maritime countries. Mixed lights of fixed and revolving sections are no longer considered satisfactory, and, what strikes the outsider as being very remarkable, colour is being gradually abandoned. Of recent years " lightning Lights " (leux clairs) have come into use, and this new system, in which the weight of the apparatus is supported on mercury, and the friction consequently reduced to a minimum, has made it possible to rotate the optical apparatus at a much higher speed than formerly, with the result that the duration of the flash has, in many oases, been reduced to one-tenth of a second.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
BACTERIA AND CHEESE-MAKING.

Bacteriological research has shown that cheese - making is a sort of gardening —an inverted gardening, in which • the plants are grown to modify the soil. In other words, the texture and flavour of the various cheeses depend on the species of moulds and 'bac- teria which grow in , them during the making. Stilton, Cheddar, Gruyere, &c., do not depend for their peculiarities on the miik, or the dis- trict where they are made. They can be made anywhere if the right bacteria and moulds are grown in the curdled milk. In the Bulletin of the Storrs Agricultural Station, Conn., it it shown that the ripening of the Camembert type of cheese is due to definite species of moulds and bacteria. One species of the former gives the cheese its peculiar texture, and it, along with the another, determines the flavour. The acidity is given by lactic acid bacteria, which also serve to retard the action of other species. It is claimed that a knowledge of the various moulds and bacteria concerned enables the cheese- maker to control the final results with greater certainty.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
A CASE OF TELEPATHY.

Professor Riche, the new President-elect of the Society of Psychical Research, has related an interesting case of telepathy,vvhich is worthy, says a writer in 2'. P.'s Weekly, of being repro- dused, since it has never before appeared in print. "I daresay you are aware, said the great physiologist, "that I was once the editor of the Revue Seientifique. Well, one day, IMMO years ago, I saw on the other side of the street (the Rue de l'Universite) down which I was walking, a friend of mine, Monsieur B—, a professor at the Lyons Faculty of Medicine. As he rarely came to Paris, this greatly surprised me. Seeing that he was walking rapidly, and being myself rather in a hurry. I did not cross the street to speak to him. 'He is sure to come to see me first thing,' I said to myself, as I hurried on towards my work. When I had been in the office about a quarter of an hour, sure enough the garcon de bureau brought in my friend's card. But, as Monsieur B-- ap- peared in the doorway, I immediately recognised that he was not the man I had seen in the street; he was, indeed, different in many ways. To make sure, however. I said to my friend: "• Were you in the Rue de l'Universite about half-an-hour ago ? " No,' was the reply, 'I have come straight from the railway-station, where I arrived by the 10.30 train.' Now, as far as I could tell, it was at that very time that I thought I saw my friend,,of whose visit to Paris, II moreover, I had not the slightest idea. I cannot regard this as a mere coincidence: it is only ex- plainable to my mind by admitting the hypo- thesis of telepathy."

AN UNCONVENTIONAL HOT IDA Y BY CYCLE AND BOAT,

3. E. CLARK, B.A. (Author of " Marvels of Ai,other World," 4c.)

Some of us, from choice or force of circum- stances, look at home for that recreative change which others search after in Switzerland end Norway, the Rhine, the, Riviera, or the Italian Lakes. Scotbind, Lakeland, and Wales may have grown familiar to us, although few will have wearied of them. Nevertheless it is well

to remember that other spots in England will repay a visit. We may recall the Yorkshire Dales, whether in the west or the less known east, the Derbyshire hills, WcTce4er's Malvern, Somerset's Emnoor. Mid North and South Devon. Familiarity with these " has afforded. many a cheering reminiscence, sometimes rising almost unbidden in pauses of busy work and city life, as refreshing as im oasis iii the desert, n the midst of the hard grind of daily toil. lint of all these visions few are brighter to the present writer than those conj tiled up by the name of Wye, even although its upper reaches remain still to him it sealed book.

Happy the wanderer who can while away A week in this charming neighbourhood, but even the scant measure of a Saturday to Monday will suffice to reveal a fair knowledge of those beauties which it shall be our task to delineate. But there are one or two preliminaries. From what point shall we come F In what season f' And bow shall we travel

Where choice is free, let " downwards," " sprhig time," " cycling," be the response. Hereford is the natural boundary between the upper and lcwer regions. There is a sense of fitness in journeying with the rapid flowing waters, and not against their tide. As to the season, in Spring all is hope and promise, the breezes are freshest, the S'aln shines brightest. THE START.

Lastly, IA to the mode of travel. Apart from cycling one has the choice of train or tramping, carriage or boat. . The first is too evanescent, carriage and boat mean cost, though it may be well to use the latter through some of the love- lier parts, when the train will help on the bi- cycle. Tramping is most independent, but means much leisure and occasional weariness on stretches of less interesting road. Cycling, too' allows a more leisurely approach to the centre of interest. As in dining, it is it mistake to attack the piece de reei.oetioe out ()I hand. Or as in a siege, the first parallels should be laid same distance off. This adds not a little to the charm. Suppose our cyclists—for two are company—come to Worcester at •evening. A pleasant hour's run will land them among the Malverns for a preliminary draught of fresh pure air. It is a charming morning's ride by Ledbury to Hereford.' First comes a good climb round one end of \ the bills. Just under the Herefordshire Beacon, a detour of a couple of miles leads through Eastnor Park. It well re- pays the extra time, for the park is very beauti- ful, and the three miles run through it is an almost unbroken gentle descent, such as may well charm the heart of any cyclist. But pause an instant at that small track on the right, a few hundred yards before the inner lodge gates and look high up into the oak. Its leaves half hide a splendid mistletoe bough. Only twc others, it is said, are knowu growing on oak ; and one of these is in Germany. We pass on through a rolling country, rich in orchard and pasture, and at last see Hereford Cathedral and Church spires before us. A visit to the former and the beautiful Castle grounds overlooking the Wye. may fitly fill tip part of our midday rest. The cathedral is replete with Norman architecture, of an earlier date than the rich- ness of its ornamentation might lead one to ex- pect. . Let us note, in passing, the peculiar wealth of the region we are to pass through in architectural interest, whether of churches, so many graced with lofty spires, or ancient castles. But even for the most indifferent, Here- ford Cathedral offers one object of peculiar in- terest, the most ancient known map, time " mappa mundi " of a 13th century ecclesias- tical, Awed on the faeta. and fancies -handed downby the ancients.

HEREFORD TO STMOND'S TAT'.

Hereford tells us of olden time in other ways. Coming from the north-west you would cycle in by Watling Street, I that legacy of Roman engineers, which later, from here to Loudon, formed the dividing line between Saxon and Dane. In " Offa's lane " on the north of the Cathedral Close, we come across the Saxon's famous dyke; to divide his folk from the Welshman, and which we come across again more than once along the Wye, until from Chepstow you can follow its course from the opposite bank, across the peninsula to the Severn.

But it is time we passed on, spending the night at Ross, whose ancient citizen, John Kyrie, has passed into fame as "The Man of Ross," on account of the many good deeds done by him for his beloved little town and country side. The Wye sweeps in beneath it with one of its sudden curves, and here, if on boating we are 'bent, is the fittest place to make our start. For just below Ross begins the region which has made the Wye most himed. But with easiy morning, let us ride on, taking the route over Kerne Bridge, the last road-bridge before Mon- mouth. The river sweeps below in a magnifi- cent bend to the left, beneath the ruins of Goodrich Castle. Our road brings us toward Goodrich Church, passing in a cutting below a tine archway in which the tapering spire is charmingly trained. Soon we leave the road for a. track leading round a hill to the ferry for Syniond's Yet. This Is a fine crag, rising some 450 feet above the stream at the narrow neck of a great bend of the river. After a course of nearly four miles the river is but 000 yards nearer the sea. No lovelier view than that . now given by the Yat can be imagined.

ISY IiOAT TO ICIONNIOUTIf.

Let us _ come down to the water, and take

boat to Momnouth, so fie to see_ the six miles of

I lovely scenery to perfection. The stream is here ' hemmed in by three counties, Gloucestershire and Hereford are to the left and right, and the hill in front, which seems to bar all progress, is in Monmouthshire. The succession of limestone buttresses on our right is the " Seven Sisters." They stand out from the cliff like pillars, or again assume a curious wall-like form, as if they belonged to the defences of one of the ancient castles. Once again we are indebted, geologically, for the beauty of the spot to the Mountain Limestone, thief tormation which, apart. from Lakeland, supplies newly all the finest rock scenery hi England. One need only mention Ingleborough, Mani Alll and (lordale Scar in Yorkshire, )iathick end Dovedale Derbyshire, Cheddar in Somerset, and the Avon gorge below Bristol. The Wye, iedeed, is ti variation upon this last. There the river has cut down through no opposing barrier, leaving vertical cliffs, 260 feet or in,,re,ni height, across which at Clifton, man has boldly thrown the finned and tilmost fairy-light suspension bridge. Here the Wye has worked along the western borders of the continuous ring, the upper edge of the basin in which • He the coal tleids of the Forest of Dean. Ti' that ancient folest, indeed, belong the trees fruiging its left bank. Once and again the river cuts throngh the underly- ing rocks as well, the ehocolate•colonred. masses of the old red sandstone. this rich tone of which comes in pleasing contrast to the grey limestone crags above. But, passing from one seeee of beauty to an- other, we are nen ii I ' B Oninonth. The rapid river has helped our lwatman. it varies much in depth. RAGLAN CASTLE.

And now, having reached and admired the picturesque little town ot Monmouth, let us make a detour to ilmrlau. a short seven talles

trwajr. winen mounts firs-P=IIE4M among _wire, castles, as Heidelberg intiong those upon the. Rhine. Its towers and halls, also, have the same - rich hue given by the red sandstone of which' they are built. It has, too, a similar mighty tower, and also bait-Gestroyect utter its last great siege, that it might net trouble the enemy again. For here, after his fatal battle at Nase- by, Charles I. found refuge with Lord Lean.- champ, one of his staunchest supporters' al- though already past his four score years. Fifty thousand men and a linnlred thousand pounds did he place at the service of his luckless- guest, lint in vain. Then came Fairfax, and the last castle faithful to the misguided monarch fell, costing its loyal owner it second slim of equal value, and a shameful imprisomnent. Let us return for the night to Symonds Yet, 'to prolong our enjoyment of its charms. The train brings us up by the river in the deepening twi- light. The scene front the balcony of the unpre- tending little hotel is still most fascinating. Far down the river the lone fisherman in his lime coracle forms a black spot among the- greys. The birds are 111:ing the Woods With their evening songs. One by one they grow silent, and now only ii single songster breaks in upon the murmur of the waters. All night long the - nightingale keeps up its tender deep-toiled notes, until with earliest dawn, thrush and blackbird nod warblers join_in chorus again.

But we must proceed. Very charmine• for the cyclist, is the ride on from Monmouth to Tin- tern, where, we see the Wye in a new phase. T:ie clear sparkling stream lots become the tur- bid river, its waters brown with the mud that can never settle in its restless ebb cud flow. For the tide at Chepstow is something to re- member. Forty feet is no uncommon rise and fall ; it may vary as much as sixty. One must go to the Bay of Fundy to get anything exceed- ing this.

TINTERN ABBEY. What an eye the old monks ever had for situation, as a rule in some sequestered valley, where the stream could provide the needful Fri- day's fish. lint of all spots thus selected few equal, none excel, that chosen for Tinter'', and its founders rose to the occasion by rearing one of the most beautiful buildings in our land. Among our ruined abbeys it stands without a peer, for fate has been kinder to it than to most of its comrades, sparing nearly all the main walls of its church, and sufficient tracery of its graceful windows to lend an airy lightnes, sad- ly missed where only heavy walls retnain.s Love- ly indeed Are the peeps of the apple orchards framed in by these windows. Lovely too had been the hushes and trees of crab and may along the edges of the stream. SPENT A LEGACY GLOBE TROTTING —William Henry Wilkinson, of Kekewick was charged with deserting hit wife Florence.—Complainant said she was now living at Leonard-stteet with her sister. Her husband had • given her no money since she was married about a year and eight months ago. A sum of £7sohad been left her by an uncle, and when she received the money her husband " wandere'd." (Laughter.) When all the money had been spent the defendant told her to "sing • for a living in town." He married her because the money had been left to her.—Questioned by the Bench complainant stated that she and her husband "had been away round the globe."—An order was made upon the defendant to conttibute ss. a week towards his 4 wife's maintenance.

A Su o CASt.—Peter Hall, jun., was charged with having on the 12th July stolen two chickens, of the value of 35., the property of his father, Peter Hall, Hollin Edge Farm, Acton Grange.—Mr. Jenkins appeared to defend the prisoner. He said he had had interviews with the father, and it was that the lad should be sent to Canada, the father to pay a second- class passage and to give him sufficient funds to enable him to start in life, and he (Mr. Jenkins) to see the lad set sail at Liverpool. There were questions on both- sides which would have to be considered if the case was gone into,and it was undesirable the Court should do, anything to embitter the relationships motel existing-. between the father and the son. He ventured to think that what he had mentioned was a sensible solution of the position.—The Bench agreed to adjourn the ease sine die on the understanding that the arrangestent mentioned by Mr. Jenkins was carried out. THEFT OF SCENT.—Elizabeth Restage (14), of Stockton Heath, was charged with having on the 17th July stolen two bottles of scent, the property of Mr. Herbert Tukton.—The complainant stated that on the day in question the defendant came into his shop and asked for some salts of lemon, and while bis back vies, turned she picked up a bottle of scent. She went away and shortly afterwards returned and asked how the salts were to be used. Subsequently he missed a second bottle of scent, and as she was the only customer who, bad been in the shop for the time he charged her ttith the theft. She admitted the theft and gave him one of the bottles.—The Bench decided to deal with the girl under First Offenders Act, and she was ordered to psi' for the scent and the costs.

A STONE THE SIZE OF A WALNUT.

A stone in the bladder the size of a walnut. What must have been the torture caused by so abnormal a growth! Few young women have been through a more

(Exact Size of Original from a photo.) terrible experience than Miss Emily Dodsley, who lives at 5, Adelaide Terrace, Downing-street, Bewell, Nottingham. Still fewer have recovered so un- expectedly and so completely froma long and dangerous ill ness. Her father and mother believed her to be dying, as well they might do ; she was suffering the doctor said from inflammation of the bladder,and had been afflicted with it for nearly a year. A slight pain in the back had been the first warning symptom. but in the end the agony became so great that her shrieks by day and night kept the house awake ; she could scarcely crawl ahout ; to stand was torture ;she could only sit on the edge of her chair ; she spent the greater part of her time in bed, sleepless and suffering. Urinary troubles were no little part of what she had to endure. She was an out-patient at— Hospital attended the dis- pensary and was seen, by three doctors all to no good purpose. At last her father determined to try the effect of Doan's Backache -.Nliss DODzL_ Kidney Pills. Both be and (From, a photo.) his daughter describe the effect' as miraculous. Miss Dodsley steadily grew better, until she was relieved of a gravel-stone of which the above is on exact reproduction in size and appear- ance. It weighs of an oz. ritcaole away without causing that torture so common with even a small stone. Since then thl cure has progressed rapidly and Miss Docisley is to-day as bright and healthy a girl as eye of man could wish to seep fiudiug it di[licult to believe in the terrible trial through which she has passed. Such is a plain, brief statement of this very remarkable case; brought before our notice by Miss Dodsley herself, and verified inan interview between that young lady, her parents, and one of our repre- sentatives.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.'

TEACIIER : "Can any of you boys tell me what the Great Plague was?" Frank: "Lessons." "Asa you in pain, my little man?" asked the visitor of a child inan infants' school. "No," . . . „

• _ answered the boy. "The pain's in me." "WHAT is the meaning of the word tantalis- ing?" asked the master. "Please, sir," spoke up Bobby Peters, "it means a circus procession pass- ir.g the school, and the scholars not allowed to look out."

"MAMMA," said little Willie, "that mean little . . • - -

Smith girl called me a monkey to-day." "Then what happened?" asked the mother. "Well," replied Willie, "you see, I couldn't slap a girl, so I gave another little girl half my orange to slap her." TEACHER: "Tom, how was this sbtte broken?" Torn: "If you please, teacher, it tumbled down, and broke itself." Teacher: "Ah, the automatic brake again." "WHAT is a zebra, Mary?" asked the teacher. "A zebra, teacher, is nothing but a horse dolt wears striped stockings all over—right up to the ears," replied Mary. TEACHER (in ,eography lesson): "Now, Mary, what are minerals?'" Mary (whose mother keeps a small refreshment stall) : "Lemonade, sods water, and seltzer, Miss." TEACHER: "Is your aunt on your mother's side or your father's side?" Tommy: "Sometimes on one and sometimes the other. It depends I n who is getting the best.of it." TEACHER: Give an example of the use of the word 'contagious.'" Pupil: "Tram-cars are not contagious; they are very hard to catch." "I HEAR that Miss Woody is inconsolable over the loss of poor little Fido." "Yes, poor thing. Ever, time it rains cats and dogs she rushes to the window to see if he is among them."

DEDUCED.—Edith: "Yes, Jack is inclined to flirt a little, but his heart is in the right place!" Marie: "Indeed! How long has it been in your possession?"

"I CAN tell you," said the sharp boy to his cottipanion, "how much water roils over Niagara Falls to a quart." "How much?" asked be. "Two pints.'MADE A DIFFERENCE.—Young Actor: "How do you like my Hamlet?" Old Stager: "Oh, it was your Hamlet. was it? I thought I did not recognise it as Shakespeare's." MISS SCREECHEN : "Papa is thinking of giving my voice a trial." Mr. Bluntleigh : "Well. I hope, for your sake, he'll not select a jury from among your neighbours." WILLIE: "Mamma, they say history repeals itself, don't they?" Mother: "Yes, dear." Willie: "Well, why don't it repeat itself when I'm trying to learn it?" BEYOND DOUBT.—Mrs. James: "My husband is so absent-minded!" Her Friend: 'What is the matter now?" Mrs. James: "He bought a sack of oats for our motor-car." 4

"My wife always takes the dickens of a time n.aking up her mind what to take and what to 101,ve behind when she goes away." "Oh, mine (Comet; she always takes all her dresses and heves me."

"Well, well." replied the great brute, "how you flatter me!" MERELY POSTPONED.—Patient: "But, doctor. only last week you said I would surely die, and to-day you see I am as well as I ever was." Doctor: "Sir, I never make a mistake in a diagnosis. Your ultimate demise is only a matter of time."

TEACHER : "Can any little boy give me an illustration of the word grateful'?" Bright Boy: "I can, ma'am. Suppose when Santa Claus comes down the chimney with a lot of nice things, he should slip and fall. That would be a grateful." A SCHOOLMASTER had been giving a lesson on physical force. "Look: here, Lucy," said the teacher to one of her bright scholars, 'you have written the word ' oyster ' without an r." "Ob. yes,' exclaimed the scholar, reaching for the slate to make the correction; "I must have been thinking this was one of those months when there is no r in oysters." "CAN'T you think of a little more!" said the teacher to a boy who had written a remarkably brief essay on Alfred the Great. So the boy stretched out his legs, and having screwed up his eyes in thought for a while, he suddenly took up his pen and wrote: "P.S.—Another good thing Alfred done was—he foundered the English

Navy." A ONE-ARMED lecturer was arguing the pros and cons of a certain subject, and, having con- cluded his summing-up of one side of the case. went on with: "And now on the other hand—" Whereupon a boy in the front row interjected: "But you ain't got no other hand ! " •• • -

A GENTLEMAN bought a new variety of potatoes, and told his gardener to be sure and plant them far enough apart. "Well, John, did you plant the potatoes far apart, as I told you?" John: "I did, sir. I planted some in your garden, and some in mine, so they are four miles apart." AT a recent examination in a Board School, one of His Majesty's Inspectors was examining a class of small girls, to whom he put the ques- tion : —"Why is it that only the King's head appears on the penny?" Imagine his surprise when one of the smaller girls held up her band. and said: "Please, sir, 'cos there's no room for his feet."

"K, double i, double t, e-n." said Mabel. Teacher : "Kitten has two • i's,' then has it?" Mabel: "Yes, ma'am; our kitten has." .

A CLASS of boys was taken in woodwork in con- nection with an evening school under a large pro- vincial Board. Among the boys was a joiner's apprentice, who for several weeks attended most regularly, and took great intierest in his work. One evening, however, he informed his teacher that he was about to give up the work. Being asked for the reason, be replied that he intended joining .tie French class, held on that particular evening, as he thought of becoming a French polisher.

A LIVING TO;ICII. Sarah Ann Banks, the Wife of a Cambt idgeshite farmer and market gardener, in consequence of domestic unhappiness went to an orchard, pouted parafin oil over herself, and then set her clothing alight, with tne result that she was literally burned to death.—At the inquest on Tuesday the husband said they had lived as "happily together es most married people.' In returning a verdict of " Suicide whilst temporarily insane" the jury requested the Coroner to rebuke the husband for the manner in which he had given his evidence.

'MOTOR NOTES.

It is stated that the Automobile Club of Great Britain and Ireland will not enter for the 1006 Gordon-Bennett. This is, without doubt one of the wisest decisions the Club has ever made. As we-have so constantly pointed out, high speed racing, either upon a specially preserved course or else% here, cannot be encouraged in a country where the speed upon the highway is limited by law to twenty miles an hour, and the only pity is that the A. G. G. B. & I. did not reach this sensible conclusion a little earlier.

In a letter to the Press, Mr. Charles Jarrott suggests that a long reliability test for ordinary touring cars, of limited h.p.,stall be organ ized next year, the idea fltow- lag somewhat upon the lines of the event arranged for competition for the tourist trophy in the Isle of Man this autumn. We imagine that some comprehensive tour would he undertaken, similar, p.rhaps, to the thousand miles tour of immortal memory in INA This would certainly be far more interesting, as well as infinitely more instructive, than any race meeting, however well-organised such might be, and it would too,be of educative value to that important but unpleas- ant personage we must all consider—" The man in the street."

The Motor Cycling Club's team trial is arranged for August 260. Every recognised automobile or cycling club in the country is at linerty to enter a " team." the prize offered being the valuable fifty g.;iness challenge cup given by the proprietors of the" Motor Cycle," , which, last year, was won by the Coventry Club. The entry fee of one guinea is sufficient for the six riders who form each team, which, by the way, must be com- posed as follows ;—Two of the machines must be single seated cycles with engines of 76 by 76, or less ; two must be single seated cycles of any power; two must be passenger machines, one at least of which most be air- cooled. The course over which the trial will be run will be so selected as to be equally convenient for all the clubs competing.

Trippers to Blackpool really do seem to have been rather hardly dealt with this year. First., motor ear races—in v.hich the three-and-sixpenny-return-ticket tripper would not be likely to take a great deal of interest—interposed between the town and the shore for two whole days, so that be on the beach found, as it were, a great gulf fixed betweon himself and civiliza- tion, and now that these are concluded a series of revivalist meetings are in progress on the beacn. From one standpoint and another, even though both may be a little extreme, Blackpool does contrive to " see life hut, nevertheless, the manner of seeing it seems to be a little hard upon the fleeting tripper.

A "practical hint " which has just gone the rounds of the technical press takes the form of a warning against the use of too much French chalk in the outer cover %then replacing a mended inner tube or fitting a new one. Too much of anything is usually a mistaken policy, but in this particular instance the fault would be on the right side,. With a superfluity of chalk there is certainly just the possibility that it may form into lumps and so injure the tube ; but with most amateurs and, indeed, with the majority of professional repairers this is a possibility 14 hi ch is little likely to resolve into a probability. The usual mistake made in this connec- tion is the use fat, far too little chalk,. a very meagre allowance being generally supposed to be amply suflicent for a tyre-repaiting outfit, if imteed, it finds a place there at all. We ourselves advocate a lavish band with the French chalk ; the trouble anticipated with too much is more imaginary that real; and, as we have said, it is hardly likely to arise with the average repairer.

An article appears, in one of war contemporaries ex- tolling the merits of the single.' cylinder engine, its chief virtue' apparently being the somewhat question- able one of failing,nt once and quite hopeless' y,directl y anything goes wrong,whilst a multi-cylinder motor will generally continue to keep going, although but slowly. By this means, the- writer argues, it is much easier to trace the fault immediately to the source. This is so, always assuming that the driver is sufficiently con- versant with his engine to diagnose the trouble correctly and, having done so, practical enough to put the matter right.

Our own opinion is that the best drivers are those who have taken their first hard lessons in motoring through the agency of a singla-cyllnder car, for these men, in order to get the best out of their engines, have I been compelled to learn how to drive with their heads, as the saying goes, consequence, they so handle I their motors that they manage to obtain the best speed of which these are capable for the minimum of wear of the mechanism. Thosebowever, who are not mechani- cally disposed, and whose conceptien of the pleasures of motoring is to pull a lever and let the engine do the rest, are best advised.to decide upon the multi-cylinder I cars even in the elementary stage of their careers, par- ticularly as there are now upon the- market some excel- kilt types at a most moderate price:. With these there is less likelihood of an absolute breakdown of the engine by the roadside, although, when this does happen, it is true that the matter of putting it richt again becomes a far lengthier job than is the case %-ith the simpler form of motor. But it is upon a vexed point such as this that the advice of a Miter Car Company's Judges should be sought when the prospective purchaser is in doubt, for in such matters much depends upon the capabilities and the mechanical knowledge of the individual, and these would be revealed to the Company' experts when the specification forms (which are filled up without entailing any liability whatever), had been duly des- patched to them for their considetation.

THE PAYMENT OF LARIIIR MEMBERS.

The objection, of a, number,. of members of the Amalgamated Society of Railway Servants to pay the levy imposed upon them for the purposes of Parliamen- tary representation has. Engineering states, led the Council to submit the whole question to counsel;the two lawyers selected being SID Edward Clarke,,K.C., rind Sir Robert Reid, K.C., ALP. These have reported that the Society cannot under the rules enforce pay- ment of the levies in question. It is pointed out, however, that, by conforming to the rules already in force, the Society may, if the- members so decide, so amend the rules, in conformity vrith usage and with the present registered rules, as to render such levies or cont ributions lawful and en forceable in the same manner as all other payments by members to the Society. This opinion is walualle to all trade unions.

T H E Ministerial NEARING THE END. chariot wheels are

still driving merrily. Mr. Bez..roun and iais supporters are panting towards the prorogation with growing misgiving about the danger of defeat in the small hours of the morning. It was not pleasant for Sir A. ACLA.ND-HOOD to find himself at half-past one with so small a reserve that he dare not push forward Rny more business. Despite the most resolute whipping the Unionists crowd keep melting away when it is long past their bed-time. Sir A. Amon:l-HOOD, whose own personal devotion is beyong praise, remarked on Thursday that he liked a straightforward fight to a finish. This was a " backhander " for those members of the Opposi- tion who lay ambushed and compel the Govern- ment Whips to ainploy scouts, and keep him in a constant fever. It may be admitted that there are few, if any, precedents for these doings. But, then, there aretno precedents for Mr. BALFOUR'S situation,:diligently as he has searched for them. No Minister has ever had such a mandate to resign. He will not heed it, and so the Opposi- tion are justified in resorting to unusual devices. A straightforward fight to a finish is the privilege of a Government that behaves itself in a straight- forward way. That is just what Mr. BAL.-TOUR refuses to do. As he will not take the opinion Of the constituencies, which are impatient to dismiss him, he has no right to complain when the party that represents in the House of Commons the majority in the country makes the closing of this azaaaing session as difficult as possible. The only indefen- sible procedure is the blocking of private members' Bills. Mr. HEALY blocked one on Thursday, and resisted an appeal from Mr. JOHN BURNS. He ' said that was the only way to obtain any considera- ' tion for Ireland, which is nonsense. Mr. REDMOND caused the withdrawal of a block a short time ago, I in deference to the suggestion that the delay of private Bill legislation meant serious loss of employment, but this reasoning is apparently wasted on Mr. HEALY. The was a fresh misfortune for the Government on Thursday night. The .adjournment of the House was moved to discuss the partition of Bengal. Lord CuazoN should have- delayed this policy until Mr. BALFOUR had reached the pro- rogation. Bengal apparently objects very strongly to being cut into two portions. At all events,the natives are protesting with great vigour. The constituencies in this country will object just as I strongly to a Redistribution Bill, which will cut I and carve them at the pleasure of a Minister who has no representative right to be in office. Lord CuazoN can do what he likes in Bengal, and no popular authority can call him to account. But Mr. BALFOUR'S account, long overdue, is sure to be presented at last.

Death has removed another of those old and honoured townsmen whom Warrington can ill afford to lose. The late Mr. William Jeffery was not a public man, but in a quiet way he contrived to do a useful work in the town of his birth. It was chiefly as a pioneer co-operator and one who had rendered strenuous service to the movement that he exercised an influence over a wide circle. The members of the Co-operative Society were rendering "honour to whom honour is dun" when less than a month ago they promoted him to the presidency in succession to the late Mr. Thomas Birchall. The Socinty has indeed been unfortunate in losing within such a short space of time vsteran. members to whose ripe experience and wise guidance it owed so much. Although Mr. Jeffery was not an active politician, he was an earnest supporter of Liberalism and all . who are concerned in the direction of the affairs of the party in Warrington will bear testi- mony to the whole-heartedness of his political faith.

Although it is not six months since the War- rington Board of Guardians deferred for a year the scheme for providing additional accommoda- tion, the members have already rescinded that decision with the result that the whole question will again be thrashed out at the next meeting. The original proposals for the expenditure of £8,430 on the erection and furnishing of a new building for the infirm inmates will be brought forward and are Rare to meet with considerable opposition. There is a sttong feeling that, in view of the high rates and not over-bright local trade, the time is unopportune for the incurring of this expenditure, and that the question rnigitt well be allowed to rest until the expiration of the twelve ' months agreed upon by a majority of the Board. Moreover, there is a divergence of opinion on the Board as to whether the £8,430 scheme is abso lutely necessary to meet the exigencies of the situation. Mr. Hickman promises to bring for. , ward an alternative and more economical pro- posal and we hope it will be carefully considered I on its merits.

During the holiday season there is naturally a lull in the local political campaign, but the Pro- gressives of Warrington will soon resume the work of educating the constituency. Arrange- ments are already being made by the Progressive Labour League for a public meeting to be held some time in September. Particular interest centres in the demonstration as the principal speakur will be Mr. L. Outhwaite who will probably oppose Mr. Chamberlain in his West Birmingham stronghold when the general election comes. Mr. Outhwaite is a colonial who has made Chinese labour in the transvaal his special subject. vßecently he visited the Cape to ascertain for him- self the true state of affairs and his words should therefore carry weight as being based on personal experience. By affording Warrington people the opportunity of hearing such speakers the Pro. gressive Labour League is doing an admirable service which will doubtless bear fruit when the time comes for an appeal to the electorate.

From time to time we have strongly advocated the full utilization of the lay-by on the Manchester Ship Canal at Walton, and there are at last indications that the Canal Company intend doing their part towards the realization of a project which many townspeople are convinced would be to the great commercial advantage of Warrington. Already the company have carried out a number of very necessary alterations at the lay-by. The immediate approach to the wharf has been much improved and the wharf itself has been consider. ably extended. The two dolphins which originally projected into the river have been connected, with the result that for almost the whole length of the wharf there is now an unbroken frontage to which vessels can be moored. The carrying out of this work shows that the Company are prepared to meet those Warrington manufacturers who are desirous of bhipping and receiving goods through the lay-by. The provision of warehouse accommodation is, however, absolutely imperative before the existing facilities can be taken advantage of. The company apparently recognize this fact, for they have cleared sufficient ground to enable a suitable building to be erected. UP to the present, however, the .ionstruction of a warehouse has not been commenced. The company have faced the greater expenditure on altering the wharf, but they appear to need convincing that they would be repaid for sinking a few hundred pounds in the erection of a waterproof corrugated iron building. Of course, a substantial brick and mortar structure would be preferable, but if the company are not disposed to incur that outlay the less expensive warehouse Pwould suffice for the time being.

It is Useless to expect local manufacturers to make systematic use of the lay-by under the present conditions. Goods kept at the lay-by for any length of time would be exposed to the vagaries of the climate—in many cases with injurious effects. Given the necessary accommodation, we are convinced that Warrington traders would not be so conservative as to continue the more cumber- some system of dispatching goods to Manchester and Liverpool for shipment and receiving them by the same roundabout means. Apart from the saving in time the pecuniary advantage would not be inconsiderable—in fact the cost may be roughly estimated at 2s. per ton loss. It is significant that one local firm—Messrs. R. A. Naylor—has found it worth while to acquire land adjoining the lay-by for the storage of cargoes of timber to be regularly shipped direct to Walton. With concerted action on the part of the Warrington traders and the co-operation of the Ship Canal Company it may not be many years before the dream of " WarringWn as a port" is in some measure fulfilled.

Warrington electors have repeatedly been asked by Protectionists to believe that England is not holding her own in foreign trade. It is well therefore to ipoint out how completely such statements are disproved by the Board of Trade returns for July. Remarkable increases are shown, in fact the record for July exports since 1900 has been broken. Decaying exports is the constant wail of Protectionists but the returns show that in July our exports reached £27,821,000 a gain of £3,037,000. As compared with the seven months January to July. in 1904 our exports for this year show an increase of £14,627,000. With the excep- tion of ships, every great branch of exports af manufactured goods shows a gain for the month. The chief increases are in iron and steel, metals, machinery, cottons, and woollens. - Another favourite contention of the Protectionists is that our trade with foreign countries is on the wane while Colonial custom is going up by leaps and bounds. The figures supplied by the Customs officials, however, shows that since 1903 exports to foreign countries, in the period January to June, have increased by £13,660,000. In the same time colonial trade has fallen off by £475,000. The latest statistics certainly make interesting and convincing reading, but whether they will be wel- comed by those who are seeking to prove that England is "going to. the dogs "' is exceedingly doubtful.

There are some peculiar notions OR Mr. Cham- berlain's campaign in America. We have been shown an extract for a United States journal which, opening with the modest statement that "once again Joseph Chamberlain appears to be coming to the surface" goes on to state: "A dispatch from London, published recently, says that people in all quarters are discussing his speech in Albert Hall reassesrting his doctrine that 'the primary duty of Parliament is to shape the fiscal policy in such a way that the working classes of Britain and Ireland shall have continuous employment.' The dispatch says: The press, without distinction of party, assents to the principle, and there is a growing dis- position to admit that only through some such re-adjustmentas Chamberiain suggests can the lot of the workingman be improved. The campaign of education has evidently not been abandoned toy Joseph." We like the subtle way in which Mr. Chamberlain's policy is here defined. The workers 'of England, however, have a much clearer but a distinctly reverse,idea as to the true meaning, of the Protectionist propaganda. As to the trend of public opinion,we wonder whether the good people across the water have.heard of the bye elections and the verdicts which all classes of the community have given on Mr. Chamberlain's "te-adjustments.!T,

Perhaps, after the immediate relief of distress' which it may afford, there is nothing io important in the Government's Unemployed Bill as therefor- ence to emigration which is enshrined in the middle of it. Money taken from the rates may be devoted to establishment charges, emigration, and the acquisition of land. Relief, beyond that, must Come from the organisation of voluntary contributions and charitable agencies. But this tentative provision for emigration links the scheme at mice with Lord Tennyson's Departmental Committee and that striking report of Mr. Rider Haggard, on colonisa- tion which is forming the basis of its inquiry. In the same way is the Unemployed Bill linked with the Royal Comm,ssion on the Revision of the Poor Law, which, indeed, it may be said, for the purposes of permanent reform, entirely to overshadow. Besides Mr. Rider Haggard, such leaders in philan- thropic movements as Dr. Barnard°, General Booth, and Mr. Booth Tucker have lately shown, in a way that is bound to arrest the public atten- tion more widely than it has yet done, what admirable work of colonisation, equally among children as adults, may be achieved if the move- ment be directed by benevolent, devoted, and experienced men of affairs.

The French Jack Tars-are having a grand dine at Portsmouth. Let us hope that the cordial under- standing which liow exists between the two great nations may continue to grow, and that the two, peoples may be drawn closer together in that noble desire—the peace of the world.

* * * The petition drawn upby the Stockton Heath Parish Council against the proposal of the County Council to, erect a new school in the district has not received that. almost unanimous support which its framers had. hoped would be extended to it. Apparently in this- matter as in the suggested boycott of the cars there is considerable difference of opinion among the' "Heathens."

* * * It is certain that Dissenters have made up their minds that they will not longer be treated as pariahs and outcasts. When a distinguished prelate of the. Church can be treated as the Bishop of Bristol has been, simply because he recognised another great Christian community as a Church, it is idle to preach to Nonconformists the duty of patient submission.— Dr. J. Guinness Rogers in the" Times."

* * A great deal is heard about physical development nowadays, but it is something new to find a writer extolling the merits of housework in this direction. If exercise is conducive to, health, says Mrs. J. S. Marshall in the "Daily Chronicle," the finest exercise that a girl can have is a little judicious housework. Every muscle' is brought into play, and as much interest can be obtained from sweeping and dusting a, room as from, a game of hockey or tennis. But what have the girls to say of this latter claim?

It: was certainly a great day for the ;seaside resorts and even the ram: did some people a good turn for the places of amusement were thronged, :and the management reaped a rich harvest. * , _ .

Ernest Allen, watchmaker, 189, knutsford-road, was on the footpath in Bridge-street On Tuesday morning when he apparently fainted, and fell in the roadway. A lurry was passing at the time, and the rear wheel pressed on his head and chest, causing somewhat serious wounds. He was at once conveyed to the Infirmary and detained as an in-patient, where he is progressing favourably. Tripper's Sudden Death. On Sunday a number of Warrington trippers visited Macclesfield. Soon after their arrival one of their number, Enoch Pessoll, of Algernon-street, Warring- complained of being ill. He went to the rear of a public-house and expired shortly afterwards. beceased had been ailing for several months. An inquest was held on Tuesday afternoon, when a . verdict of "Death from natural causes" was returned.

At a meeting of the Executive Committee of the • Warrington Trades and Labour Council it was decided lhat no attempt be made at the present time to bring about an alteration in the Corporation contracts fair wages clauses.—At a meeting of the Council on Tuesday, Councillor Stott said it was felt that it would not be advisable to interfere at the present time though the clauses were very unsatisfactory and were very little improvement upon the old standing order. It was felt that it would be hopeless to attempt to bring about an alteration with• the Town Council constituted as it was , at Present. The committee would, however, have certain reconnnendations to make before the arrival of the November:elections.—lt was decided to write to the Prime Minister, the Home Secretary, and Mr. R. z ierpoint, M.P., protesting against the action of the Hera eSecretary in refusing to meet representatives of the Scottish Women's Trades Council with regard tO the conditions of labour in the laundry business. ,_PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST OGOTS x LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Choir Trip. The members of St. Clement's Choir (numbering about 30) held their annual excursion on Monday when ..tiey visited Morecambe. The party were accompanied by the Rev. T. W. Barber (curate-in-charge), Mrs. Barber and several friends from the district, and later _O,O were joined by Mr. Hodgson, of Lymm, brother of the late Rey. D. S. Hodgson, first priest-in-charge at t..st. Clement's. After an excellent luncheon, which was served before noon, the party proceeded by char-a-bane O Halton when a brief inspection was made of the interesting old church, at present undergoing restora- tion. On resuming their journey the party travelled to Caton, where games of various kinds, walks, &c., formed a settifig for an enjoyable afternoon. An excellent tea and a charming drive to Morecambe, the weather throughout being fine brought to a close one of the most enjoyable excursions ever experienced by the choir. A vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Fred Horton, who (in the absence of the curate-in-charge) Made the arrangements for the day's excursion.

Death of Mr. Richard Silk. '1 The death took place at his residence, 20, Beamont- street, on Saturday evening, of Mr. Richard Silk, the well-known coal merchant. Mr. Silk had been in a declining state of health for about 12 months, and his death was due to consumption. He leaves a widow and five children to monm,his loss. Peculiar Accident. A singular accithnt occurred on Wilderspool Cause- way on Sunday, John Lowe, cab driver, in the employ of Mr. H. F. Greenough, cab proprietor, while driving a cab along the Causeway passed an electric car the trolley pole of which snapped and dropped•on his head. Death of Mr. Edward Gibbon, of Lymm, Mr. Edward Gibbon, of Manor House, and formerly lof Yew Tree Farm, Lymm, died on Sunday after a long illness. Mr. Gibbon, who was in his 68th year, was a successful farmer and breeder of shire horses, , and carried off prizes at various shows. He was at one time farm bailiff for Mr. George Charnley Dew- : hurst, grandfather of the present squire, and also carried on business as a corn miller in the village. He was a Churchman and prominent Conservative. MA.rt. CARTS and Bassinettes, the latest designs. J. S. GARLAND. 51 to 53, Bridge-street, Warrington. —ADVT. Two Boys Poisoned by Laburnum Pods. On Sunday evening Detective Clough heard a com- motion in Orchard-street, and on going to inquire the cause, found that two lads, William and John Harrop, aged 10 years and nine years respectively, sons of Mr. Joseph Harrop, of .23, Orchard-street, had been brought home ill. They said they had been eating laburnum pods, which had apparently poisoned them. The lads were very sleepy, and great exertions were required to keep them awake. An emetic was administered, and Dr. Anderson sent for, and the lads are now reported to be progressing satisfactorily. Fire at Colborne-Street Chapel. On Wednesday evening the Rev. Joseph Ellison was conducting the weekly service at the Golborne street Baptist Chapel, and while engaged in prayer he was informed that the building was on fire. The congrega- tion quickly dispersed, and the fire brigade was sent for. In a short time the flames were extinguished the fire itself did little damage, but the church fittings, carpets, and books were damaged considerably by water. The fire was caused through the ventilator above the central gas chandelier becoming blocked. The services will be continued to-morrow (Sunda)) as. usual.
