_ We record with regret the death of Mr. Thomas Whittaker, of Grappenhall-road, Stockton Heath, which occurred under painfully sudden cirenmstances on Wednesday. Deceased had for the past 20 years been a sufferer from asthma and bronchitis and on Wednes- day afternoon he visited the surgery of his medical adviser, Dr. Reginald Smith, where he died suddenly while consulting the locum tenses. At one time he was employed by tile Wigan Coal and Iron Company as a traveller, and subsequently by Messrs. Fletcher Russell and Company, retiring about twelve years ago He was a staunch supporter of the Warrington Foot- ball Club and was one of the most regular attenders at the clubs engagements. He was 5g years 'of age and leaves a widow and one daughter. The funeral will take place this (Saturday) afternoon at the Cemetery at 3 o'clock.

Temperance and Agriculture. During the four days of the recent Royal Lancashire Agricultural Show at Wavertree, Liverpool, the Diocesan Branch of the C.E.T.S. had a large marquee with a frontage of 25 feet. Drinks suitable for use at haytime and harvest were made and distributed free along with recipes and other literature by a staff of twenty workers, under the direction of. the Secretary. It is estimated that no fewer than 15,000 persons visited the marquee and tried the drinks and 20,000 copies of recipes &c., were distributed. Mr. Henry Taylor, our local Missionary, along with Mr. Gladstone was responsible for the linking of the drinks. The whole staff of the Societywith the Rev. J. Hirst (secre- tary), worked hard to make the event a success. WASHING AND WRINGING MACHINES try J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55 Bridge-street, Warrington.— ADVT.

Motor Car In Collision. A collision between a motor car and a cart occurred on Wilderspool Causeway early, on Sunday morning. Alfred Cook, broker, 322, Knutsford-road, Warring- ton, was driving in company with six other men in a horse and cart, and Mr. Thomas (rat ton, of Stockton Heath, was driving a motor car in the same direction and in endeavouring to pass the cart his car skidded on the tram lines and ran into the back of the vehicle. The horse was knocked down and the shaft broken, Cook receiving slight injuries. Mr. Garton was accompanied by two other gentlemen, one of whom, Mr. Robert Reynolds, Burscough Bridge, near South- port, was thrown out and received a scalp wound and other injuries. He was attended by Dr. Collins and afterwards taken to the infirmary, where he is pro- gressing most fatisfactorily. OWING TO CONTINUED ADVANCE IN PRICES. The GROCERS and PROVISION DEALERS are com- pelled to advance the prices of Butter, Cheese, Bacon, &c.—Advt.

Warrington Field Club. On Bank Holiday, despite the threatening morning, a number af members of the Warrington Field Club travelled to Hartford and had a day's ramble by Petty. pool, Abbot's Moss and Kernnel Wood. The whortle- berries and lilberries were not quite ripe. Two species of eundew were noted. Many moths, beetles, flies and spiders were taken, amongst the latter a rare hunting spider, Lycosa herbigrada, not hitherto recorded in the district. This will find a resting place in the local collection at the Museum. Careful recot ds were made of the birds and mammals that were seen, the most interesting of the latter being a squirrel, whose anxiety to escape the obsenation of so eminently scientific a party led to his rapid rush up a birch tree from whose topmost boughs he scrutinized them. in a very clinical way. After partaking of tea at the Blue Cap the party set out for Hartford, but, being over- taken by a severe thunderstorm, sustained a drenching which somewhat marred, the pleasure of an otherwise delightful ramble.

;New Burtonwood Organist. I Mr. Arthur Pennington, of Ashton-in-Makerfield has ! been appointed the organist and choirmaster of St. Michael's Parish Church, Bartonwood. He is a pupil of and has been coached for the position by Mr. Charles Henry Dawson.

Cruelty to a Canal Horse. At the Newton-le-Willows Police Court on Thursday, William Twigg. of Widnes, was charged with cruelty to a horse, at Earlestown, on the 3txr inst.—Sergeant Barrow and Inspector Tunes gave evidence to the effect that the horse was attached to a boat on the St. Helens and Sankey Canal. • On the right hip was a wound six inches square, on the left one of three inches square, under the collar was a complete mass of raw suppurating wounds, the horse was painfully lame in alt legs from laminitis, and immediately it was put in a field it fell down, and had since been under the care of the police. It was stated that the horse and boat belonged to Mr. Richard Abel, Oldhall- street, Liverpool.—Mr. Linaker, for the defence, stated that when the defendant took the horse out he did not know it was lame. Defendant was fined 20s. and costs.

LADIES' BEST GLACE BOOTS AND SHOES, UNDER THE CLOCK, BRIDGE-STREET.—Advt. Fires on a Cheshire Farm. On Tuesday at Runcorn, Michtel Kelly, formerly of Widnes, and now of New Ferry, MIS charged with being found on the enclosed premises of Robert Shepherd at Parkside Farm, Aston, for an unlawful purpose at 4.30 a.m. on Sunday. The prisoner, it was stated, was found asleep under the hay bays in the stackyard, and how he had got there was a mystery, as there was a barbed wire fence seven feet high round the premises. There had been eight fires at this farm within a short period. At the last fire some thousands of pounds damage was done. These fires generally occurred between Saturday night and Mon- day morning. Matches had been found on the prisoner and it was asked that the heaviest possible penalty should be inflicted. After heariu the evidence, the magistrates sentenced the prisoner to two months' hard labour.—The Prisoner: You should send to New Ferry for my character,

Accident on the Transporter Bridge. About nine o'clock on Tuesday nigh't,a somewhat seri- ous mishap occurred on the Widnes and Runcorn Trans- porter Bridge. As the ear was approaching Runcorn, over the Ship Canal, a large plank fell from the girders overhead, a height of about 80 feet. The car was fairly well loaded with passengers, and the plank just grazed the head of Mrs. Stringer, of Suffolk-street, Runcorn, struck Mrs. Mary Williamson, of Brackley- street, on the shoulder and arm, and then alighted on the foot of her daughter, Miss Mary Williamson. A Widnes young lady, whose name has not yet been ascertained, was also struck, and fainted. The Williarnsons and the young lady were taken to Dr. MacLennan's surgery, and were attended to by his locum tenens. The mother and daughter had t') be taken home in a cab, and are now confined to bed. Their injuries are considered to be serious. The Widnes lady was able to walk back to the car with assistance, and make the return journey. Mrs. Stringer is suffering frcm shock.

Next Thursday's Agricultural Show. The Sixteenth annual show.of the Wan ington and District Agricultural Society will be held in Walton Park on Thuisday next. Given fine weather, this popular event should prove exceptionally successful. There is a record entry, numbering 453 in the 51 classes, There is 41 splendid entry in the shire hackney and harness classes and a keen competition may be expected. Prizes, cups, (Sic.. are offered to the value of about £750. As in past years, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., gives a district champion cup value .£.lO and two silver cups value £5 each and other special prizes are offered by Lady Greenall, and Mr. F. Stapleton Bretherton. Mr. J. Whittaker Kenworthy,Mr. James Gould,Messrs. Henry Milling and Co., and Messrs. Day. and Sons. Medals are offered by the Shire Horse Society, the Hunters' Improvement Society. A special feature of the show will be novice classes in the open hunter and open harness classes for horses not having previ- ously won large prizes at other shows. The Yorkshire Hussars Band will play a selection of masie during the (lay. 'Buses will run from the town to Walton at frequent intervals. The showyard opens at 9 o'clock, and judging will commence at 9.30. Sir G. Greenall will give a lunch, and on this occasion the public will have the opportunity of joining it. There will also be a tea tent.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 12, 1905.

LATEST NEWS.

Japan's Peace Terms.

Japan's peace terms are now in the possession of the Russian Government. The terms were presented in writing at Thursday's meeting of the peace pleni- potentiaries, and were cabled in cipher to St. Peters- burg after being considered by the Russian envoys and delegates at Portsmouth, United States. After Russia has considered the terms she will make her reply, also in writing. The sittings of the conference stand adjourned until such time as the Russian reply is ready to be submitted.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LANCASHIRE COTTON DISPUTE.

Possibility of Settlement. In some circles, a certain optimism prevaili that the dispute in the Lancashire cotton trails may yet be settled without a strike. The majority of notices tendered by Stockport spinners ran out on Thursday night, but it is taken as a hnppy augury that an arrange- ment has been arrived at between the masters and men whereby the spinners will continue to work until nnon on Saturday, when the mills will close for a week for the annual wakes holidays, which commence on Monday next. _

Mr. Boner Law, in the House of Commons on Thurs- day, said that if the Board of Trade had reason to believe that the intervention was likely to prove nseful they would at once take steps under the Conciliation Act, MO. The neatest interest in the crisis is, perhaps, being felt in Bolton and district, where notices for 5 per cent. reduction have bean received. Mr. Gill, the cotton operatives' secretary,interviewed on the situation, says there is no jmtification for a reduction at the present time, as ihe Bolton trade is in an improving condition. The margin of profit last week was seven-sixteenths more than the margin in 1888. in 1900, when the masters were making fabulous profits, spinners made application for only 5 per cent., while the employers were in a posi- tion to pay 20 per cent. -

INCREASE OF WAGES GRANTED. Three large cotton-spinning firms in the Ashton-under- Lyne district yesterday morning conceded the advance of 5 per cent, asked for by the workers.

Electric Train Disaster.

Jury's Finding on the Conduct of the Signalman and Driver.

- . The inquest concluded on Thursday,at Blundell Sands on the 20 victims of the Hall road electric train disaster on the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway on 27th July. The motorman, William Rimmer, and the signalman William Boote, both gave evidence. The former said Boote " flagged "him through, and noticing the points were set for the middle siding, witness shut off the current, but had not sufficient room in which to pull up. The signalman said that when the express came up he forgot to reverse the points. He lost his head when he discovered the mistake. Over evidence showed that the signals were in perfect order after the collision. The jury found that the accident was due to a mis- take of Signalman Boote and Driver Rimmer through an error of judgment, which was censurable but not criminal._

lney recommended that the " Leine " points should be conversant with the working of the motor, and that signalmen and drivers should be periodically examined as to rules.

Cricket Professional's Suicide

The inquest Wa4 held at Haslingden on Thursday on the body of John Usher, a well-known cricket profes- sional, bodylwas taken from the reservoir the previous day. His widow said the deceased was engaged with the Crompton club up to the last Saturday in July. He had been in trouble a long time, and said he would not live through it. When he came home a week last Saturday he said things she did not care for. She did not see him between Friday morning and Tuesday night. The police stated that the body did not appear to have been long in the water when got out at ten o'clock on Tuesday forenoon. The Coroner said it was common knowledge that the deceased had been in trouble lately. The jury returned a verdict that TJ she! drowned him- self when, by reason of his adverse circumstances, he was not of sound mind.

THE VOLUNTEER ENCAMPMENT. AMUSING EXPERIENCES.

[BY OUR, PECIAL COnnESPONDIn'T.] Fii PAY.—Friday was spent in general skirmishing, the general scheme being a sham fight between the right and left half battalions. A strong wind was blowing from the south-west and the effect on the men, was ludicrous in the extreme. It speaks well for our fatigue Party when it is stated that all our tents stood the fury of the gale. Squally rain fell at intervals. A serious accident occurred to a Warrington 'butcher named William Evans, in camp. He was holding a piece of meat when Sergeant-Butcher Baldwin accidently crashed into his hand and wrist with a cleaver, splitting them open. He was quickly attended to by Surgeon-Major Adams and is now doing well. A shooting match took place between the olficersiand sergeants, the non-com.'s winning by 4 points. Captain Avery won the first prize in the officers' competition with 72 points, Lieutenant Pickton being second with 67. A concert was held in the large marquee in the evening when a good pro- gramme was given by a number of variety artistes.

A 600 D DRENCHING..

Sawana.Y.—Satorday mornieg breke fine and clear, and the men were early astir. After being once more medically exmined,tents were struck, everything packed up, and put away. The boards were then well scrubbed and left to dry some twenty yards from camp. The men then paraded an shirt-sleeves and practised skirmishing and field signals in the vicinity. After about an hour's drill, rain commenced to fall in torrents. What a rush there was for camp I The men worked as probaby they had never worked before. The tents were thrown up in quick time and the beds, kits, &c., pitched in helter- skelter. However, we took it all in good humour and soon settled down to dry ourselves and our clothes. Great amusement was caused by the following inci- dents :—Two horses belonging to the Mounted Infantry had somewhat trayed from camp. The cyclist company were sent out in search of them, ancl,not being able to find them, the men hired a couple of hobby horses from a fair an the neighbourhood. These were brought back to camp and concealed in the cyclists' lines and Captain Avery was sent for. This officer quickly came on the scene and, although he was fairly "had," he regarded the matter as a hugh joke. General Booth and his staff passed the camp in seven motor cars on their way to Sviudon and wefe heartily cheered by those who saw him. A TRIP TO SOUTHAMPTON AND THE ISLE OF WIGHT. SUNDAY.—The only parade on Sunday was the church parade, at which the chaplain, the Rev. NV. Bracecanap. officiated. After breakfast, a large contingent proceeded by two special trains to Southampton, some stayed here and were interested in the decorations which had been displayed in honour of the French fleet. The rest had a delightful sail round the Isle of Wight and saw the King's yacht and several battleships in the Solent. Three services were held in the Y.NI.C.A. tent, the Rev. W. Bracecamp preaching at the evening service on "Playing the fool." A SPOILED FIELD DAY. MONDAY.—On Monday the commencement of a series of manceuvres should have been made at 8 a.m., but owing to rain, there was a postponement. The battalion paraded at two o'clock and marched off with filled water- bottles and haversacks. A sharp march was made through Tintown and past the Perham Down camp to Warren Hill. Here the men were engaged in outpost duty. The-idea was that the oetposts were on night duties and the battalion were instructed in the duties of sentries, visiting patrols. reliefs, &c. On the way home, the battalion whistling band," gave some lively tunes. In the evening the opening match was played in the first round of the football competition "E" Com- pany beating the" M.T." by two goals to nil. A HARD MARCH AND A WARM DAY. TUESDAY.—An interesting day's work was gone through on Tuesday. It was supposed that a red force consisting of the Herefords and Cheshires,the section of the South Lancashire Mounted Infantryand the section of R. F.A. under Colonel Scobie had been raiding the coentry, and a blue force under Colonel Wynne Edwards and consisting of the South Lancashires and Welsh, 3 sections Mounted Infantry and 1 section R. F. A., had only been partly successful in repelling the attack. The orders for the blue force were to regain their transport and then to proceed along the road to Marlborough from Bulfird. A very hard march of about six miles under a blazing sun brought us to the scone of operations and a very stiff fight ensued. Three accidents were repursed during the day, Sergeant Magian of the Mounted Infantry having the misfortune to break his collar bone and two of the cyclists having very nasty spills, damaging themselves and their mounts. The battalion returned back to camp about 2.20 p.m. having been out since 7.20 a.m. The two remaining matches In the Ist round of the football competition\were layed in the evening and resulted as follows : H" Company, 1 goal," C" Company nil ; "B "Company 2 goals," G " Company nil. VOLUNTEER PHYSIQLTE. As the result of the medical inspection in accordance with the terms of the recent War Office circular, it is stated that not a single man in the four battalions of the Thirtieth Field Army Brigade failed to come up to the standard of requirements for foreign service. This is a unique record. The volunteer battalions which have the distinction of forming the Thirtieth Field Army Brigade are the Ist South Lancashire (Warrington and Newton-le- Willows), the and Cheshire (Chester, Runcorn, Ac.), the Ist Royal Welsh Fusiliers (Wrexham, Denbigh, &c.,) and the Ist Herejordshire.

SUCCESS OF THE BLUE FORCE. WEDNESDAY.—Yesterday's operations resulted in the Red Raiding force being driven back to Shipton Derringer where they reinforced. This morning the battalion paraded at 7.30 in shirt sleeves with 20 rounds of ammunition per man and marched along the road (?) past Tidworth to the rendezvous. The enemy were dis- covered advancing in column from the east front, apparently from Parkhouse. The left flank of the Blue force changed the direction of firing and the Red force were apparently repulsed. At the time of writing, the umpires' decision had not been announced. The weather in the morning was very threatening, but with the exception of one or two slight showers, the rain kept off. The battalion arrived back in camp shortly after 3 o'clock when dinner was served. Three matches wore tlaved in the second round of the Cup competition; "D" company gained a victory over the Cyclists by two goals to one, "A " and "I" played a drawn game of one goal each, while were defeated by "F" to the tune of two goals to nil. The massed bugle bands of the brigade, assisted by the 3rd V.B. West Yorkshire Regiment, sounded "Retreat" to-night under the, direction of Sergeant- Drummer Plinston. THURSDAY'S OPERATIONS.

-- The operations on Thursday were again on n very evtensive scale. The Cheshires, South Laneashires, and Yorkshires were opposed by the Welsh and Herefords and a stiff fight ensued. The Mounted Infantry, Cyclists and signallers have been made use of during the opera- tions and have acquitted themselves very creditably. H.R.H. the Inspector-Ceneral of the Forces is expected to visit the camp on Friday to inspect the lines and men's kits. Kcourt of inquiry is to be held with regard to several accidents in order to grant compensation to the men. The general health of the men is good although there have been a number of rather serious accidents among the cyclists end Mounted infantry. TIME OF RETURNING. The battalion will return home abotic 7 o'clock this (Saturday) evening.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

I Warrington have struck a vein of bad luck just at present, that is if their continued reverses can be attri- buted to the eccentricities of Dame Fortune. Since June 14th they have won one match and prior to that date three clubs only had proved unequal to the task of either defeating them or sharing the honours of the encounter. To win four matches out of seventeen played does not enhance the reputation of the club, and to SO7lO of their supporters the metaphorical last straw was brought into operation when they were again defeated at home. Still the majority are not without hope of seeing them win ere the season closes. So far that pleasure has been denied them as every match on the Arpley enclosure has resulted in either a draw or a defeat.

Saturday's match was an important one inasmuch as it had a great bearing upon Warrington's position in the League table as it was tlope,d that by winning the local eleven would ensure the avoidance of the possession of the wooden spoon. They did not win and consequently there is a strong probability that they will occupy that ignominious position. Their only rivals are Prescot and they hold the advantage of having a game in hand while only a single point In arrears. The position of the clubs up-to-date is:— P. W. L. D. P. Earlestown Stanley Newton 9 5 2 ... 2 ... 12 St. Helens 9 .. 4 ... 3 ... 2 ... 18 St. Helens Rees Warrington Prescot

! Saturday's match attracted a large number of spec- +ators to the Arpley ground, and batting first, St. Helens totalled 134. There was a time when the task of equal- ling such a score would have been regarded as a light one by Warrington, but now, apparently, it is completely bevond thorn, and thy replied with the insignificant total of 76, out of which Nlason and linwtin scored 36, each recording 14 runs. The only other players to reach double figures were Bibby (10) and Podmore (12).

; Bibby was the only player on the Warrington side who gave a good account of himself as in addition to making a comparatively respectable score he took four of the St. Helens wickets forK, runs. Thewlis captured three for 31. The batting averages as a result of the poor display suffered all round and are now Times Most 1 not in Inns, out, inns, runs. average E. A. The second eleven figured with no more success than their seniors and were soundly beaten at St. Helens, The home tea n compiled 109 runs, half the wickets falling to E. Pierpoint, and in reply Warrington made 73, four players only reaching double figures. -- Mason, the popular Warrington professional, will, at the close of the season, sever his connection with the club. He signified his intention of doing so recently and endeavours to settle the differences which have arisen have proved futile. This will be widely regretted by supporters of the club.

Walker's Brewery team have been busy of late and the last two matches played have resulted in victories. On Wednesday they defeated the Widnes police by scoring GO for seven wickets in reply to a total of 52. The previous Saturday they triumphed with ease over Haydock. Batting first they scored 128 fgr the loss of four wickets, Dargan heading the list with 44 not out. Haydock were dismissed for 74. --- Stanley came a surprising " cropper" at Earlestown thanks to the efforts of Crowther and Robinson who between them scored 90 runs out of 109.

Sankey Wire Mills do not, apparently, believe in do- ing things by halves. Prior to Saturday's match they were undefeated and had inflicted some severe trounc- ings upon their opponents. Oa Saturday they visited Hindley Green, and g tve a surprising account of them- selves. Batting first, they were altogether unable to judge the delivery of Spicer and Battersby, and one by one had unusually brief stays at the wicket. The highest score was 6by Taylor, and eight players between them recorded 10 ! Spicer took four wickets for seven runs and I3attersby 5 for 12. Battersby was also prominent in the butting, compiling a vigorously hit 53.

Moore and Dareshary and the Ctble Company's team had a close fight on Saturday, and in the end the last- named club won by ono run. Moore batted first and their innings was a triumph, of W. Howard, who took seven wickets for 16 runs, doing the " hat trick." -- Grappenliall second eleven entertained Arley on Saturday and won easily. Hallows took eight wickets for twelve runs.

Generally speaking bowlers held the upper hand in Saturday's matches and the two excellent records stated above were equalled in the match between Latchford Baptists and St. James', Groarke and fieesom taking respectively four of the Baptist wickets for two runs and four for three.

Bowling. Pearson and Knowles No. 1 team have realized their highest anticipations and for the second year in succes- sion are the proud possessors of the Finlayson Cup. They have not come by that honour without a struggle and throughout the season they have displayed consist- ently excellent form which has earned for them the well deserved little champions of the works teams. The final they did not view with any misgivings and the result—a handsome win—justified their confidence. Fletchers, on the other hand,recognized that in the con- cluding stage of the competition they were faced with an unusually dqicult task, but they did not view the match as hopeless and while they were defeated they were not disgraced.

The King and Queen green, over which the match was played, was in splendid condition, thanks to the atten- tion of Mr. Rogers, and the climatic conditions were such as conduced to good trundling. In anticipation of witnessing a keen contest a large number of spectators assembled, nor were they disappointed. From the large margin of points which separated the teams it would perhaps appear to be otherwise but the contrary was the case the bowling of both teams being of a high order. J. Hardman and J. Bridge set the ball rollingand Fletchers man triumphed after loading throughout. R. Rothwell atoned for the initial defeat sustained by his side. He and J. Massey were level until the eighth end when a double gave the "forgeman " the advantage. Massey tried hard to retrieve his position but Rothwell always held the upper baud and ultimately gave his side an advantage of two points.

The two captains had a lengthy fight. Peeling at four Ball ran to ten. Evans then secured the jack and the score was carried by him to 8-11. This, however, proved his concluding effort, and he had to admit defeat without improving his position. Flotcher's at this point had a run of ill-luck, and their outlook was the reverse of having improved, when W. Ashall was beaten 15-5 by T. Hatton. Ashall never once looked like winning. H. Bennett alsoproved too strong for his opponent. Open- ing with a single he scored nine before atintith opened his account. For the remainder of the game the points were equally divided.

W. Mee was equally successful in his game with J. Noden and P. and R. led by 37 points with six players off the green. Fletchers men fully realized the serious- ness of their position and began to assert themselves. Hackett was the one to set the lead and he did not meet with success until he had been given a bad frights. At the ninth end he was four points in arrear to NV. Bowering but securing the jack he equalized with two doubles taking the lead immediately afterwards. Bowering did not retire without a struggle but he had met more than his match and the second win was recorded for Fletchers.

C. Littlewood and F. Lleesom had one of the most, exciting games associated with the fixture. Littlewood i opened n a brilliant manner and at the third end led by five points to nil. A single to both players made little alteration in the situation but Fletcher's arm having got into the run of the green entered into the reckoning. Three doubles made him equal and again securing theminimum points for a single end he led 10-8. Two singles further improved tals position when the forgeman again asserted himself and peeled. Two ends later the scores were again equal. The last point was hotly contested and it was secured by Heesom after a splendid bit of work.

Fletchers had begun to entertain hopes of success hut they were doomed to disappointment. The defeat of J. Massey jun., making it practically impossible for them to triumph. Massey's play was surprising. He com- menced indifferently and was rapidly left behind the scores at the twelfth end being 1:3-4. He then ross to the occasion and secured the next seven points. The improvement however came too late and he was beaten.

P. Ball maintained the family traditions and after a somewhat uncertain opening peeled at seven. Taking the lead he went to 13-9 when he fell off. Broadhurst secured the jack and elect- ing to ray on the most tricky part of the gre3n obtained the lead. He got his first wood in the last end well placed but Ball beat him by a few inches and P. and IC. continued in the ascendant. J. W. Worslev bad a long fight with E. Palley and it was not until the 23rd end that he claimed the game. The pair peeled at four and five and against 11 and 12. Interest

One must hasten to congratulate the winners upon their success, and I cannot say more than that it was richly deserved. I must also sympathise with the losers who also played well and deserved a better fate. No doubt they will be again heard of in future contests aarl I for one am confident that they will ultimately succeed in their ambition.

Naturally after the match there was a little speech- making and this took an appropriate form. The prizes were distributed by the Mayor who intimated that in his earlier years he was a follower of bowls. Since then however his interest has displayed itself in other directions and he has not been in active association with the game. After Saturday it is to be hoped that his sympathy will reawaken and will display itself in the Council Chamber if not in the advocacy at any rate in the sup- port of the extension of municipal greens. He handed the cup and gold medals to the winning team and silver medals to the runners-up.

An excellent suggestion was made by Mr. Gibson, of Pearson and Knowles who advocated the construction of bowling greens on the lawn in the park. While I would not go so far as he did and support the pro- visionlof a green in front of the Town liall, there is land sufticent in the rear of that building to cater for the requirements of the residents of the centre of the town. Mr. E. Taylor, secretary of the Warrington and District League, paid a compliment to the committee of the Works Bowling Association and words of praise were never better deserved. The com- mittee with Mr. J. Willett AS its chairman have engineered the competition splendidly and in less capable hands the season would not have been so suc- cessful. They are not however resting from their labours as they are engaged arranging for an individual merit competition, the success of which is already assured. The endeavours of the committee would how- ever be of little avail were they not backed up b 3 an able secretary and in Mr. Smalley the association has the services of an official who at the same time is the most popular and capable the organizationhas possessed

The Warrington and District League matches which were played on Saturday had an unusuaal bearing upon the positions occupied by the clubs in the competition. In the first place the Bull's Head players further strenghened their position while the Horse and Jockey men rendered their ultimate occupancy of the fourth position very uncertain and perhaps even improbable. This vas the outcome of their defeat by the champions on the Jockey green—a most surprising result. While they did not view the em...ounter with any assurance the Church-street men saw there was a possible chance of victory and by taking advantage of every little slip , made by their opponents they improved upon the total of the home team by 19 points.

The principal contributors to this success were A. Shaw alli R. Fairbrother who in their games secured a balance of points which exceeded by one the final margin. The game throughout was sternly contested and the Bull's Head team have every reason to feel proud of themselves.

The Horse and Jockey men had a busy day on Saturday as in addition they entertained the players from Penketh Park. The game had the expected end- ing and Penketh Park for the eighth time this season had to acknowledge defeat. Ont of the games played only two were won by their representatives. Cycling. An imp3rtant case affecting cyclists was heard at Leigh on Thursday, when a cyclist was charged with riding on the field footpath leading from Mather Lane to St. Helens road, Leigh. Mr. Dootson. who defended, pointed out that cyclists had a legal right to ride on field footpaths, hut not on footpaths by the side of highways. He claimed the advocate's fee against the police. The case was dismissed, and ths bench allowed the dsfence one guinea advocato's fee.

The unsettled sta.to of the weather during the past week his militated against open air enjoyment and con- sequently local cyclists have not been much in evidence. On Saturday members of the Warrington Cycling Club set out on various week-end tours and the outings proved highly pleisurable. Qu Tuisday some 15 mem- bers turned out for the club run to Cronton, the even- ing proving an enjoyable one.

On Thursday the wheels were discarded, and the club indulged in a cricket match with an eleven selected by Mr. \V. H. Podrnore. Rain stopped play when Mr. Podmore's eleven had been dismissed for 96. The principal scorers were Loy (2i), Brooks (16), and Bailey 'ham° (10). Mr. Isaac's innings was a particularly exhilarating one. Taking his stand at the wicket he scored tan oil the first throe balls which he received, and was dismissed with the fourth.

To-day's run iq to Oulton Park, and on Tuesday Grintsditch Mill will be visited.

Football.

Preparations for tho coming football season are now well in hand, and Warrington players will turn out for the first practice on Wednesday next. Hallam has again been elected captain of the first team and when he i 8 absent the doings of theplayers will be directed by Fish Knight has control of the second fifteen. Six new players will be tried including Bibhy and Lloyd War- rington youths who figured with success in last season's works competition.

The annuli sports in connection with the club were held on August Monday when the large attendance on the Wilderspool enclosure testified to the popularity of the event. The programme wan a highly interesting one and many well-known runners and riders figured in the competition. General regret was felt at the absence of Hyman, of Pensylvania,the world's champion 220 yards sprint racer, who was unable to turn out owing to a strained knee. Hyman's accident was par- ticularly unfortunate as he was unable to compete in the championship races whice were held at Stoke on Tuesday. Broughton Rangers have secured three new forwards in D. Bell,forrnerly captain of Morecambe: E. Darlison, of Featherstone ; and E. J. Gage, of Exmouth and Penarth.

Hull King-ton Rovers are negotiating with Swinton for the transfers of To Samuel. Evan Vigors, and Dan Rees.

The Salford and York clubs are aain in negotiation for Vernon ifarnpson's transfer, and the Salford club are in negotiation with Swinton for the transfer of "Joey " Morgan. .

Annual Show.

The members of the Hatton Horticultural Society hcld their annual show at Hatton Hall on Thursday,the exhibition, in spite of the unsettled state of the weather being a great success. The number of entres showed a gratifying incroase over those of last year and the quality of the exhibits also showed improvement. As in past years the vegitable classes were very strong; as were also the floral exhibits. The farrn produce classes showeda decided advance and everything tended to show that the society is doing a useful work. The exhibits were artistically arranged in marquees and all round called for honourable comment..

The Hen. and R.w. C. F. Cross over the opening ceremony, at which there was a large attendance.

Mrs. Lyon again performed the ceremony and spoke of the pleasure which it afforded her to be present. She eulogised the work of the Society and the excellence of the show especially praising the table decorations. Mrs. Lyon was heartily thanked for her services on the motion of the Rev. T. T. Lee Jones, seconded by Mr. T. Ditchfield and votes of thanks were also accorded Arr. Parr for the use of the field, Mr. Hatton for the able discharge of the secretarial duties, and the judges for their service; and the donors of the prizes for their generosity. The judges were :—Farmers' produce, Messrs. .1. Stokes and Edwin Britch ; cottagers' garden produce, Messrs. J. Parkinson and R. Poulton; hones', Mr. H. Potts ' • butter and eggs, Airs. J. L. Jones; table decora- tions, Mrs. Lyon, Miss Whitley and Miss Blair; baskets of flowers, Mrs. Wyatt and Miss R:dgway.

NATION GROWING SOBER.

Interesting Facts From an Official Report.

The 48th report of the Commissioners of Inland Revenue for the year ended March 31, 1905, issuad on Wednesday, is a highly interesting document. The report shows in particulars that the nation is becoming more sober every year. The beer tas, it is shown, yielded £13,101,459, and the spirit duty £18,135,931. Both duties give decreases as compared with the previous year, the former of £359,822, and the latter of £531,887. Statistical proof is afforded of the fact that Scotland is not a beer drinkinr, nation, for it only contributed £618,735 to the total of the duty on that product sa compared with £11,214,126 from England. Ireland gives double the amount of Scotland. England paid duty on 29,385,208 barrels, Scotland on 1,813,109 barrels, and Ireland on 3,205,970. The following table for the past six years, giving the estimated consumption of beer per head of the popnln- lation in the United Kingdom, shows that beer is losing its popularity : Gallons per Person. 1899-1900 1900-1901 1901-1902 1902-1903 1903-1901 1901-1005

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THEFT FROM THE WORK FIOCSE. At Warrington/ on Monday, Thomas Harp, last from ' the workhouse, was sent to gaol for one month with hard labour for absconding on July 19th from the work- house with the Union clothing. It was stated by the porter that a coat and vest belonging to the Union, which the prisoner was wearing when he absconded were found in an outbuilding in Bostock-street.

_ STREET BETTING AT WA REINGTON'.--I‘IAXIM Ulf

PENALTY INFLICTED.

M the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday, Henry Lockett, 26 Winwick-road, was charged with frequenting and using Stamford-street for the purpose of bettinv..— Defendant pleaded gnilty.—Superintendent Monaghan stated that on the 19th July Detective Clough and Meacock we- e instructed to watch the defendant. They did so from 1.10 p.m. until 1.45 p.m., and during that time saw him take slips and money from 59 men and four boys. Defendant made entries in a book. He had as many as eight persons round him at one time, and during the period in which he was under observation he was visited by over 100 people. Defendant had been betting in that part of the town for some considerable time and had been once driven out of the neighbourhood by the police. He asked the magistrates to deal severely with him and so get him out of the neighbourhood.— Defendant, who had nothing to say, was fined £5 and costs, the maximum penalty.

PROSECUTIONS OF POACHERS. At the Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, before Mr. Nield (in the chair), Colonel Ridgway and Mr. Monks. Charles Packer and Henry Ellison were sum- moned for having been found in possession of certain implements and nets used for the purpose or taking game at Hay-lane, Walton Inferior, on the 23rd July.— P. C. Preston stated that on the day in question he was in the company of Constables Stewart and Edwards of the Warrington police force, when ha saw the defend- ants in Hay-lane. He stopped and examined them, and found two nets, two bludgeons, 14 pegs, and one bag in their possession.—The defendants assured him that the bludgeons were used for the purpose of getting over- hedges and ditches. (Laughter)—Superintendent Ennio° ; They Vo oilld do for something else ifthere was only one policeman.—Ellison : I think we are too old for this game now,l think w. shall have to chuck it.(Latighter). They are too smart for us.—Ellison was fined 15s. and costs and Packer 10s. and costs. —.lames Davidson was charged with a similar offence at Walton on the 23rd July. Ihe case was proved by Sergeant Railow.— Defendant said he was sorry he had committed the offence, but if he had not been in drink be would not have been there. A fine of 20s. and costs was inflicted, Richard Hughes, who failed to appear, was similarly charged and was fined 408. and costs. the Chairman re- marking that he had a bad record.—lt was decided that all the poaching apparatus be handed over to the police to be destroyed.

Warrington Borough Pollee Court.

Minor Cases.

MoNDA Y.—Before Dr. Fox (presiding), Messrs, WI Richardson, H. Thornton, and C. Jackson Holmes. Ttre,Fr OF SWEETS.—Three boys. Charles William Dawhorn, 60, Haydock-street ; Martin Broderick, 10, Erwood-street ; and William Cunningham, 29, James- street, were charged with stealing from the shop of James Riley, 23, Orford-lane, between August 7th and fith, a quantity of sweets, valve s.—Superintendent Monaghan said that the prosecutor saw the shop locked up at eleven o'clock on Monday night. There was a pane of glass broken in the window, but another pane had been temporarily placed behind to cover the hole. Shortly after five o'clock that morning, Sergeant Smith, in company with another o;licer, was in Horsernarket- street ; they saw the prisoners, who turned up Queen- ' street. The eldest boy was caught, and be made a state- ment, which led to the arrest of the other two boys, and sweets were found in their possession. Sweets were also fonnd on the footpath outside the prosecutor's shop.—Dawborn, who had been brought up last year for sleeping out, was sent to prison for 21 days in the second division, and the other two boys were ordered to receive twelve strokes each and be kept at the Police Station until six o'clock.

THE WIZARD'S GLASS.

(Conrtglit.)

BY AMY MILLER,

Author of "The Silver Veil: A Mystery,• "Love Knots and Fetters," "The Slave of the Lamp," &c.

Graeme Castle rose, frowning and dark, amidst the surmunding woods, more like a prison in aspect than a home, at all seasons. Especially grim and sorrowful it appeared on an evening late in the year when the wind wailed amidst the leaf- less branches of the trees, and the leaves lay under- "foot, sodden and decayilv. Roland Murray paced the winding paths restlessly. .The desolation of the scene and the melancholy of the hour were to him only emblems of all his life to come. The exquisite promise of the spring, the brief gladness of the summer were swept away, and winter was upon him. The sun, he thought, would shine for him no more. Only the memory of its light remained, sad and pale, as the fitful moonbeams that shone at intervals from the cloudy sky. Be was not yet twenty-five, and he believed that this night would give him the last few moments of happiness t\at he would ever know in this world. Anxiously his eyes turned towards the grey old castle. Now all was dark, and now the pallid moon chewed him the ivied tower, the long terrace, and the gateway—nothing more, till he became sick with hope deferred. The clouds grew heavier; the moon remained hidden. He only listened then, and the sound he waited for came not.

"Is it possible she will fail me after all ?" he cried, bitterly. "Oh, it was too much to hope that she, the worst injured, would trust in my innocence, when so many call me guilty." A sudden stream of silver light, a rustle of footsteps on the fallen leaves. With a faint cry that was almost a sob, he sprang to meet the figure that hurried towards him—a girl's figure, graceful and slender, that he clasped in a passionate embrace. The sweet face was raised to his, the soft arms clung about his neck, the lips gave him back his kisses as never till this hour of sorrow. When last they had met alone she had been a light-hearted girl, coy and shy. To-night she was a woman, who could remember nothing but that they loved each other, and that this was their farewell.

"Roland, 'have yon waited for me long? Dearest, I dared not let them suspect. They would have prevented our meeting." "Oh,__Lilias, my darling, I was selfish to ask for this. Yet I could not go away. I could not leave you for ever without taking with me the memory of your voice, telling me that you believe me guiltless." would not believe you guilty if all the world said so. I know you are innocent. I know the truth will one day come to light," the girl answered, steadily. "Pray Heaven it may. But, even so, that day may be far distant, and, oh, my only love, there is one thing I must say to you." "Not yet—not yet," she interrupted. "You are going away, Roland; driven into exile, by unjust suspicion. . _ _

"‘I can bear it no longer. It would be braver to stay, I know, but when, in every face I recognise, I see, even where it is thought concealed, the horror felt for a man whose hands are stained with blood —when my former friends have grown cold, and even strangers seem to regard me with suspicion— ah I how can I go on with such a life?" "But you are innocent, Roland—the law declares you so. _

"The law cannot, prove me guilty, and so I am free ; but the world believes me guilty. There is only one way to endure my fate. In a strange land, myself a stranger, bearing another name than my own, I may forget--" he paused, and added, with a sad emphasis, "and be forgotten." Those words fell on the ears of Lilies sorrow- fully as a knell. "No, no!" she cried, and clung closer to him: "Roland, Roland," she whispered, "you are going into exile. Take me with you! Let us wait, together, for happier days." He did not answer for some moments, but she felt the heaving of his breast, and a tear -fell on her upturned face. "Hush!" be murmured at last. iLilias, you must try to forget me!" "Oh," she sighed, "you cannot love me, or you would not ask it! What is left to me but p-i? I have lost my brother, I have lost the home that I loved now that strangers share it with me. You are my only hope. You alone can comfort me. And you ask me to forget you?" "Because I love you. Let me not feel, Lint's, that I have made all your future desolate. If any suffering of mine, if the sacrifice of life itself, could blot the past from your memory, you should remember nothing. You are too dear to me to share my tarnished name. Think of me, when you must, as of one whom you will never see again. To-night, though I shall live, Roland Murray dies —Roland Murray, who loved you, and died happy because you trusted him!" Lilias uttered a low cry of anguish. He kissed her solemnly, as he might have kissed the dead. All her pleading could not weaken his resolution. The truth was revealed to her. It was for her sake that he was going away. So at last they parted. She went back to the dark, fortress-like building, which once had never seemed gloomy, whose ancient walls had echoed to her girlish laughter. He went slowly away, through the dark woods, each step taking bun further from her, each turn of the path recalling some memory; for here was the scene of his life- drama.

The glade, where in the summer time the flowers bloomed and the brook sang gaily as it rippled over the stones, had been the lovers' favourite trysting-place. The avenue beyond was where he and Oswald Graeme, her brother, had met and quarrelled bitterly. As Roland 'went on he lived that brief, but fateful interview over again, till a more terrible recollection took its place. He' had reached the very spot where weeks before he had found Oswald lying dead. Oswald, from whom he had parted in auger. The gun with which he had been shot lay near ; Roland had it in his hand when Cyprian Talbot and others of the shooting party found him. He had been accused of the murder. The jury had decided in his favour, but the shadow of the crime hung over him yet. The true murderer of Oswald Graeme had escaped suspicion. Roland Murray, who had loved the sister of the dead man, and to whom the brother had refused her hand, WIAS the only person, said the world, who had a grudge against him, and Roland was known to be of an impulsive and passionate disposition. Certain threatening words of his had been overheard. had been found with the discharged gun in Ms hand, leaning over the murdered man. Perhaps the only one who did not regard him with doubt was Lilies.

More than two years had passed away, and the freshness and gladness of the spring made Paris, like all the rest of the world, newly gay and fair. In a luxuriously appointed room, bright with sunshine and dowers, a painter was at work at his easel. The room was not a stud,o, but part of a suite recently engaged by an English family. rhe girl whoss picture was growing under the artist's hand was rarely beautiful. Slender and tall, graceful and fair, she had been aptly named Lilies, and her poetic loveliness was worthy of portrayal by such a master as Gustave Lenoir. Paris, and the art world generally, raved of him and of his work. He was a man of thirty-five, of a most striking and picturesque presenee, and possessed of singular ch,rin and power. Though by birth a Frenchman. he had some Italian blood in his veins, and in Italy he had perfected his teemical skill and learnt many secrets of 1113 art. His pictures, marvels of form and colour as they were, yet owed their chiefest attracton to the weird fascination and subtle suggestiveness which he :rive to them. It is not every great painter who is, like Gustave Lenoir, a poet and a mystic as well. His portraiture was remarkable. He appeared able to penetrate the mask of cc4tom and reserve. "It is the soul he paints," a s ,aid of him, and the', though he did not alw.ys please, he invariably interested. Lilies Graeine, his present sitter, appealed no less to his artistic perception then to his heart and mind. The exquisite image iiron the canvas was a triumph. Yet it did rot seem to meet with the approval of a young man who entered quietly and stood regarding it front behind Lenoir. He was several years younger than the painter, and in all respects his contrast—fair, foppish, with shallow, shifty eyes, thin lips and narrow forehead, darkened just now by a frown. The painter, feeling his presence, turned a dark intellectual face towards him. "Miss Graetne's portrait is almost complete," he said, in perfect

U 0 I see. I should like it better had you painted her as yon did young Lady Vesey." Lenoir smiled. "One cannot paint one person like another, Mr. Talbot, or it would not be a portrait." "Lady Vesey looked the picture of happiness. My cousin looks —" Ile paused. An elderly lady, who had been sitting in the background, rose and joined the two men before the easel. "it's very pretty, very sweet," she declared. Lady Talbot applied the same adjectives to the masterpieces in the Louvre. "But a little odd. That strikes you at the first glance, Cyprian. I admit I thought so at first. Now I can see nothing I could wish changed." Lenoir listened impassively. Lilias rose to end the sitting and interrupt the discussion. Looking from her to her portrait., the genius of Lenoir was revealed, though in a manner, doubtless, scarcely pleasing to the feelings of a lover— especially such a lover as Cyprian Talbot. The haunting pathos of the violet eyes, the mingled wistfulness and patience that spoke in the pictured face, were in the otiginal for those who had eyes to see. Yet the living girl was only a young lady, beautifully dressed in modern but artistic fashion, surrounded by all that wealth can give, and too young, too carefully guarded, it might be supposed, to have any knowledge of sin and sorrow. -And the girl in the painting was a maiden-martyr of romance, looking, out from the shadows of grief and oppression, longing for the aid that does not come. "Waiting" would have been an appropriate title. Soon after Lenoir had taken his departure, Lady Talbot slipped away, leaving her son and Lilies alone. The girl-sat beside the window, looking listlessly out on the stream of carriages and pedestrians, seeing them not. Cyprian came to her side and took her hand. It was instantly withdrawn, and she flashed at him a look fell of reproach and resentment. "Cyprian," she said, "you make my life unbearable!" "Your own folly does that," he answered, savagely. "Are you going to waste all your days in sentimental regrets for a man who was never worthy of you? You profess to have loved your brother, yet lie was unwilling you should be that scoundrel's wife." "Roland's only crime was poverty," Ulf.. cried. "Dare to malign him to me!" "And you were rich even before he killed Oswald." She rose and would have left him, but he caught her slender wrist in a vice-like grasp and held her fast. "Let me go," she exclaimed, the colour rising to her cheek.

"Not till you have heard what I have to say. Then let everyone see what Lenoir has seen, if you like, but deny me any longer at your peril. I have been patient, but I will be patient no longer. Be my wife, Lilias, or "—he bent forward hissing in her ear words that turned her cheeks to an ashen pallor. "No, no!" she moaned. "It is not true I" "I swear it 1" "Cyprian, have you no pity ? " "None, unless you consent to be my wife."

THE SHIP CANAL.

Shareholders' Half-Yearly Mating.

A SATISFACTORY SIX MONTHS.

The half-yearly meeting of shareholders in the Ship Canal Company was held on Thursday. Mr J.K.Bythel chairman of the company, presided over a small attendance.

in moving the adoption of the report and balance sheet, the Chairman said in the Ship Canal they had an increase of 119,000 tons in the weight of sett borne merchandise and £10,197 in the receipts as compared with the first half of last year, this was the more encouraging because the half-year did no tbegin well. The January and February receipts Jell off Ito the extent of £3,380, hut after that the trade turned, the deficit was rubbed off, and the present position was reached. The ii.crease in expendi- ture was £2,094, but as £l,BBO consisted of rents, rates, and taxes, almost entirely owing to the new large storage warehouse in Trafford Park-road having come into use, the expenditure on balance showed very little increase. The Bridgewater Canal traffic and receipts were favourably affected by the renewed activity in the cotton trade, and the net revenue was £14,700, against £11,688, an increase of £3,012.

THE NEW DOCK AND PRESENT PROSPECTS. Nine fully-laden ships had already gone into the new dock, and be thought the successful opening ceremony might be taken as a happy omen. Manchester, he thought, was now recognized as having established one of the leading ports, which was bound to hold its own in face of competition and continue to .progre-s. The policy of providing increased accommodation and equipment had been justified by the result, and he was perfectly certain it would be justified in the future.

THE_ COTTON TRADE.

So far this season 720,000 bales of cotton had been shipped from America and Eyypt to Manchester. During the same period of last season they had 511,000, and in the season before that (which was the best so far) they had 623,000. In the banana trade, and in other direc- tions also, there had been very satisfactory developments during the half-year, and they felt satisfied that steady progress would continue to be made. Alderman Southern, in seconding the motion, said the half-year had been by no means one to discourage those whose hope of the undertaking had been sustained so long. The children stood looking at their new pets, And suddenly they noticed a very strange thing —a number of moths' wings, like the wine of the newcomers in the box, lay scattered about. They counted six on the bench and ten on the ground. How did the .wings come there? The new moths were quite perfect, every one having its two pains of wings. 07" - .17— bodies to be seen, only

.intside there were no wings, wings, wings I What had happened? "Chirrup! chirrup!" said a saucy-looking robin cin- a \ neighbouring tree. Another of the brown, moths flew past, almost brushing Peter's nose. The new-corner flew to the box, settled on the muslin, and seemed to be saying good-morn- ing to the prisoners. Peter and Phccbe stood still, wet:thing. Whir-r-r-z! Down came Mr. Robin. In a second he had snatched up'the kind moth in the middle of the call, gobbled up the body, and left one more pair of brown wings to explain how all the other brown wings came there.' Peter and Phcebe told the robin how naughty he was, but he only looked saucier than ever. The children let the new moths fly away, and tucked in around their looking-glass the wings of the loving and unfortunate callers. IN THE HAY-LOFT. Up in the hayloft—kitten and It With a window open to the sky, Curtained with boughs of the chestnut trees That toss and sway in the cool west breeze. The dome of the sky with a cloud is lined, And the rain comes down when it has a mind, Pelting the leaves of the chestnut tree: Never the rain can touch kitten and me. Up in the hay-loft—kitten and I! The hay behind us is mountain high; The teanas across are dusty enough; Darkness broods in the peak of the roof.

lii peazly lines the daylight falls Through the chinks of the boarded walls; . .The air is fragrant- with clover dried, Brake and •daisies and things beside. Queer little spiders drop down from on high; Scftly we welcome them—kitten and I! wallows chirp in a lazy strain - Between the showers of the summer rain. • The strange word with its contradictmy meanings is "bound." The boy was "bound' to go swimming, bat had he been "bound " he could not have gone. he held a book which was handsomely "bound." The school- girl could • "bound" every hedge in Derby- shire.

___+__ PUZZLES FOR PLAYTIME. Numerical Charade.

My 7,1, 9,5, is a lady. My 5,9, 2,8, is a. girl's name. My-3, 4,5, 5,1, 5, is a rivulet. My 7,6, 5,8, 2, is a vision. My whole is a well.known port of Holland. Square Words. The god of war. A medicinal plant. A girl's name. An animal. Riddle-me-ree. My first is in ROSE, but not in THORN. My second is in NIGHT, but not in MORN. My third is in ORANGE, but not in PEAR. My fourth is in WOLF, but not in BEAR. My fifth is in DAY, but not in NIGHT. My sixth is in WRONG, also in MGM'. My seventh in in ROBE, also in DRESS. My eighth isin FOLLY, but not in JESS. My while is a beautiful ficwer. Double Acrostic.

(Finals,' as well as initials, form a word. linals give a certaid gum tree; initials, a shrub). A mineral. A specie of confectionery. A boy's name. A vegetable. An eelozne. A girl's name.

•FOR AN INWARD LAUGH. A visitor in the country seeing a very old peasant woman dozing at her cottage door, asks a little boy of six or seven, who happens to be playing near by, how old she is. "I can't say, sir," replies the child, politely; "but she must be very old. She has been here ever Since I can remember."

HOW DID THEY COME THERE?

The Maudy family always keep a box full of caterpillars and worms. Isn't that funny? But, you know, these creatures turn into queer things called cocoons. In this form they live for many days until their little houses open and they come out butterflies or mottle. This year the Maudy family expected moths of the kind called Polyphemus. One morning Peter and Phcebe Mandy went out to the box, which they kept in the garden, and in it they found four of the beautiful brovOnish moths just out of their cocoons. There they were, fluttering their wings for joy because they felt the warm sun- shine for the first time, and troubled only be- cause the thin muslin over the top of the box

MORNING COMPLIMENTS. '44 light little zephyr came flitting,

Just breaking he morning repose. The rose made a bow to the thy, The lily .she bowed to the rote. And then, in a soft bah, whisper, As faint as a p(rfume that blows: "You are brighter than I," said the lily; You are fairer than I," said the rose.

Two young men out riding were passing a farmhouse where a famer was trying to harness an obstinate horse. " Won't he draw?" said one of the men. "Of course," said the farmer, " he'll draw the attention of every fool that passes this way." The young men drove on.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Proposed Appointment of Resident Medical Officer:

The .Bishop's Visitation..

The Bishop of Liverpool hopes to hold his visitation in November. Instead of delivering a single charge to the clergy in the Cathedral,he purposes to divide it into eight parts, and to deliver them in eight centres. The visitation for the Winwick rural deanery will be held at the Parish Church,Warringtomon November 27th. The Bishop proposes not only to cite the clergy,but also to invite the attendance of the churchwardens, sidesmen, licensed lay readers, ruridecanal representatives, Sunday school superintendents, and head teachers of the day schools.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
Memorial Service.

A large congregation assembled at the Hill Cliffe Baptist Church on Sunday evening to take part in a memorial service to the late Mrs. Hellas, of New Lodge, Higher Walton, uho for some years was an active ruember of the church. The Pastor (the Rev. J. S. Hughes) occupied the pulpit and took for his text the passage "Willing rather to be absent from the body and to be present with the Lord." He dealt with St. Paul's explanation of a Christian's death and his concep- tion of a Christian's heaven. Referring to the deceased lady, he spoke of her many amiable qualities, the great esteem in which she was held and the devoted service she had rendered to the church up to the time of her breakdown in health. For over two years she had been in a weak condition, and yet her comparatively early death had been a a surprise and sorrow to them all, and as a church they suffered a severe and unexpected loss, Mrs. Hanes would be ever remembered amongst them, as one who gave of her very best to the service of that church, which she fondly loved. At the close the Dead March in " Saul," was played the congregation standing.

Re-decoration of Holy Trinity Church. The last service in the above church prior to closing for re-decoration was held on Sunday evening when the Vicar announced that the church would be closed until Sunday, September 3rd. The services would be held during that period in the schoolroom in Museum- street at 10.30 a.m and 6.30 p.m. each Sunday. Holy Trinity Parish Magazine says :—" The expense of painting and decorating the church will be £l2O. Towards this amount there is about £9O in hand, leaving a balance of £3O still to be raised, which it is hoped will be wiped dff by the collections at the open- ing services. While the church is undergoing decoration the wardens and the Parish Council considered that it would be a good opportunity to have the lighting of the church overhauled, a work that has for a long time needed to be taken in hand. To do the work effectively it was felt that a very considerable outlay would have to be incurred, and it was decided to start a special fund for the purpose. Our churchwarden, Mr. J. H. Halton, has given a great deal of thought and time to carry out this work, and we are very pleased that his efforts have been well responded to. We believe that the work when completed will leave nothing to be desired."

BANANAS. Every fruiterer's shop bears witness to the growing popularity of bananas. Two record shiploads of these are now due—one at Manchester, of 57,000 bunch(As, front Costa Rica, in the steamer Pecuare, and the other at Bristol, of 45,000 bunches, from JBMIIiCA, in the steamer Oracabessa. The first represents the largest cargo of bananas ever loaded in one steamer at any port in the world, and the second the largest ever shipped from Jamacia.
