LETTERS TO THE EDITOR

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
The Grand Duke.

Sir,—The writer of "Observer "notes, in the local Conservative organ, overstepped the bounds of common decency again last week by his unwarranted attack upon Mr. A. H. Crosfleld. One expects the Liberal Candidate to be abused because of his political activity, but one scarcely anticipates an attack upon him because of his personal friendships. "The Grand Duke Mich2el has everbeen a thorn ia the side of the "Observer " and, if we were to take serious notice of that paper, we should be led to believe that be was a really dreadful individual to have as a friend It now turns out that the" dreadful individual" I actually patronised a Primrose League flower show and garden party, nay, the gathering was, so the" Observer " stated, " under the immediate patronage of his Imperial Highness and the Countess." Well ! really it is funny ! What will the " Observer " do now? If I may offer some advice to the writer of the notes 'referred to, and he surely is in need of advice, I would humbly pray him to read the wise words of Confucius, who, once upon a time, said that "if you suspect a man, do not employ him; if you employ him, do not suspect him."—Yours truly, NUNQUALM.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Christian Festivals and Their Dates

Sir,—More than one day in seven are observed as Sabbaths by peoples in the world. This has caused me to consider which of these Sabbath days are held on the right day. I have come to the conclusion that the Sabbath day in England is fixed on the wrong day of the week, at some future time I will give you my reasons for thinking thus. Those who fixed the dates of Easter and Christmas were much astray in their reckon- ing. I believe the birth of Christ occured in the summer time. The argument I use to prove this is that the shepherds were watching their flocks by night. This proves it was not winter time when Christ was born. In Palestine flocks would not have been in the fields in Enid-winter at night. It seems rather absurd that Easter should have been fixed three weeks later this year than last. It appears to me that the dates of Christ's birth and death are lost and likewise the dates of the week on which He died and rose again. Then why do people pretend to know these affairs? I should like to see Christmas and Easter abolished. Bacehusat these times seem to have more worshippers than Christ. I would suggest that Christ's Day should be held in place of them and all public house closed on that day.—Yours,ecc., R. M. B. Grappenhall, August tith, 1905.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
National Training and the Public

Schools.

Sire—ln accepting a gold medal, commesnorative of his presence at the Bisley meeting on the 23rd of July His Majesty the King expressed his warm interest in rifle shooting and said" I am glad to think, and trust also, that the boys in our different schools will now take up rifle shooting." This royal encouragement to rifle shooting, as part and parcel of the education of British boys in all schools, come's as a happy confirma- tion of recent notable utterances, such as the remark of the Prince of Wales that " training of the young is the foundation of every sound system of national defence " and the splendid appeal to the nation made by Lord Roberts in his speeches in the House of Lords on July 10th and at the Mansion House on the Ist inst. These things lead me to believe that your readers will be interested to hear the steps which the National Service League has been taking to bring home to the boys of England, and especially to those in our public schools, the responsibilities which reet on every young Briton, personally to prepare himself to share the honourable burden of national defence in the day of need. It has from the first been part and parcel of our proposals that military drill and rifle shooting should be made an obligatory part of the educational course in all schools, public and private, primary and secondazy. The National Service League contends that such training, while imbuing boys with a sense of manly discipline and true patriotism, would enable them to carry out their military training proper much more easily and at much less sacrifice of time than would be necessary if no gronnd work had previ- ously been laid. With this conviction we felt very strongly the urgent need there is for bringing our principles as clsarly as possible before the youth of the country, as it would be of inestimable value to the great national purpose we are "pursuing to have the sympathy and support of public schools in urging upon the rising generation the necessity of performing the first of all civic duties. With this object in view the Executive Committee took steps, some weeks ago, to offer to all public schools which would make physical and military drill obligatory on all boys and rifle shooting on all boys above the age of 15, a gold and silver medal, to be awarded annually to the best trained boys in the school the idea being to pick out, andilionour, "the best soldier," in the best sense of the word. I am now able to say that we have received answers from nearly all the public schools, and they have been favourable in nearly every case. This fact, indicating as it does, the complete acceptance of our principles by the headmasters of 90 per cent, of the big schools, show that it would be easy to carry a bill making inilitary drill and rifle shooting compulsory as the groundwork of a truly national reserve. The following schools have accepted our offer at once: Uppingbam (the first school in England to make Rifle Shooting obligatory on all boys) Rossall, Stonyhurst College, Merchiston Castle, Edinburgh, Warwick School The King's School, Chester, The King's School, Worcester, Bromsgrove School Worcester, Sutton Valance School, St. Edward's School, Oxford, The Schools Derby and Trent College, Long Eaton R.S.O. The following schools have stated that they hope to be in aposition to fulfil conditions of the offershortly Marlborough College, Christ's Hospital Radley College, Bradfield College, Hymer's College, Hull and Blackheath School. Several important public schools have not been able to give a definite reply, owing to changes of headmastership or similar circumstances. It seems to me that the ready response to the idea of national training for national purposes, indicated by these facts, is full of encouragement for those who are earnestly striving to make the citizens of this Empire realise their great responsibilities for its honour and security. I trust, therefore, that those who believe in the ideal of national service will give us their hearty support by joining the League, and widening the basis i of public opinion n its favour to such an extent that this, the most urgently needed national reform, may speedily be carried" by the free will of a free people." —I have the honour to be, your obedient servant, RAGLAN, President of the National Service League. Dacre House, Victoria-street, S.W. August :t.ll, 1905.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON AND THE SHIP CANAL.

The Company and their Dredging Obligations.

APPEAL TO BE PROSECUTED.

In the course of his speech at the half-yearly meeting of shareholders in the Manchester Ship Canal Company, the Chairman (Mr. J. IC Bsthell) referred to the Com- pany's Warrington dredging obligations. He reminded the shareholders that at the last ordinary meeting he stated that the Warrington Corporation and the three trader plaintiffs had appealed to the House of Lords against the judgment of the Cosrt of Appeal, which was in the Canal Company's favour on the point of juris- diction. The Court held that the matter in dispute ought to have gone before an arbitrator. The House of Lords, while confirming the decision of the Court of Appeal to the effect that the Corporation of Warrington were in the wrong, and must be struck out as plaintiffs, held that the three traders were entitled to bring an action without previously resorting to arbitration, and ordered that the traders' case should go back to the Court of Appeal to be tried on merits. The Company's appeal, he added, will be prosecuted.

===== 0002601_19050812_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
MONEY MADNESS.

THE INDUSTRIAL REVOLUTION IN AMERICA.

The South African Stores Scandals.

[By Mr. H. Whitham.]

As I ventured to prophecy a few weeks ago revolu- tion in the United States seems to be close upon us. Of course I think the people have sufficient good sense to see to it that the revolution shall be one resulting from good active brain work, some thing that will prove to the world that the so-called governing classes having been weighed in the scales and found wanting must now make way for the masses. The order of things is changing, the Industrial Revolution is on, indeed it has reached an acute stage, and unless we are to have a repetition of the French Revolution those who have and wish to retain had better loose' their hold, or—No better object lesson could be given to the world than the one given by Russia during the past twelve months. It is not only the governing class that has proved its alter incapability to govern the country and at the same time proved to the world how utterly corrupt it is, but there is another class that delights in bellowing out its patriotism,.a class that is always conspicuous for the respect it pays to privilege, and the worship it gives to vested interests, a class that always thrusts itself where the loaves and fishes are, and minds to secure the Government contracts. Like the Russian captain who starved his men and deservedly paid the penalty with his life, these men from the Grand Dukes to the contractors, right away down step by step all give way the moment, they run against the economic side of affairs. Fifty years ago the workers did very little reading and thinking, , indeed there is much to be desired on these lines at the present day,and they were only able to get hold of scanty information about the methods pursued by the Army contractors. The supply of iron bayonets and paper shoes Tommy Atkins knew all about, but when he wanted an overcoat to shield him from the bitter Crimean winter he had to whistle for it. Economics got the best of the contractors' patriotism. So it is in every war, and yet the proletariat will enlist and put themselves under the control of these flannelled fools., During the Spanish-American war, the patriotic con- , tractors supplied embalmed beef, and to my certain knowlege drugs that would have poisoned pigs all to "get rich quick" A few weeks ago I received from a London friend Sir W. Butler's report upon the system by which Army stores were disposed of in South Africa at the close of the war. I have read it, and what a blow it has given to my love for my native land. Sir William has had the courage of his convictions. Not being a Tory, but a Liberal, and an officer, it is pretty certain that he has been often voted as " no dem'd good," and would most certainly be outside the charmed aristocratic circle. As the Liberal candidate for a Leeds division, I sincerely hope for his success. It would seem that by the adroit manipulation of the tenders, and the very brotherly relationship existing between the contractors and the Government afficials the British taxpayers were " jewed " out of a nice sum of money. "In fact, the " jewing " that led up to the war, and the " jewing " that figures in its closing scenes makes one see clearly that the Gentile is no match for the Jew when economies are in the question. The highest legal authority in the Transvaal describes the various " loose and inconclusive documents" given to the officials as "underhand tenders." The first information received showed the clever scheme of fraud which cost the ' country from four to five millions sterling. In a letter from Colonel W. Pain commanding in Bloemfontein is this real bit of Yankeeism. "As regards this station, Messrs. Myers and Co., pay Government lls. per 10e, lbs. for oats and 10s. per 100 lbs. for hay, and though the stuff is not removed from the supply depot, they sell it back to Government at Ns. 4d. and 13s. 7d. per 100 lbs. respectively." I have no intention of troubling the reader with more instances of the interested patriotism of the British Army officials. It is enough to use his own words and leave him in the bands of the voters. His words are: "When we sell a consignment to a random purchaser he re-sells it— to us—at a profit of from 50 per cent. to 500 per cent." In reading over this report of General Sir William Butler's I am reminded of our own economic troubles in this land of the free, America. I have given in rseveral preceeding letter, readers of the " Examiner" brief and very incomplete sketches of the American economic and industrial condition. I have barely referred to the alarming condition of the Insurance Companies. Insurance is now so general among all classes that it behoves one to be very care- ful as to what is said touching the management of the various societies engaged in the business. To a large extent they are international in the business they do. The working classes are much interested in their good and honest management and the law is very strict and definite, as to what shall and what shall not be done with the vast savings that flow through the hands of the managers 'of the various societies. It was therefore a distinct note of alarm to the people, nay, a very thunderbolt that fell at the door of millions of the best class of citizens when Mr. Tom Lawson in his campaign of "Frenzied Finance " spoke of the consolidation of vast sums of money in the hands of bankers and insurance companies and its use by Wall-street speculators for the plundering of the people. The truth of his statement is' now being largely confirmed by the recent revelations concern- ing the Equitable and other great New York corn-I panies. It has been given in evidence that the I Equitable has been lavish to the degree of criminality in its use of the peoples' money—in the giving of banquets, in the wasteful spending of money for its officials to travel about the country in Palace cars, in the office furnishings, and most of all in salaries paid to dummy officials, and to officials who do exist, but whose heaviest work is drawing their salaries. Men ' who have hitherto held the very highest social ' position the nation had in its gift were not proof against the economic conditions that afforded them a chance to rob ,the people. The Hon. Chauncey M Depew, a Senator of world wide reputation, a man in request at every national and State function, a very prince of after dinner speakers did not scruple to gather fruit at the Equitable insurance plum tree. As a consulting lawyer, yet one who could spend most of his time travelling over the world, he has been picking up his 20,000 dollars a year salary. Well may the question be in everyone's mouth—" Has business honour vanished"? Seemingly among men of the various highest social standing there is no sense of responsi- bility to others, nor yet does there seem to be any ethical code whatever. What is called " easy " money is what all are looking for. Directors holding no stock, and dummy directors are pocketing their fees , for meetings they never attend or can attend. Eminent I statesmen, senators, governors, prominent journalists all are in the gras ping business and looking for loot. New York is fast becoming the financial centre of the world, and its business men, there can be no doubt of it, are men with no sense of honour, their methods are those of the Standard Oil, the Steel Trust, the International Shipping Trust, and the Copper Trust. At least seven of the heads of the Beef Trust are now indicted before the Courts. If there is a convic- tion the only penalty that can be inflicted is a fine of £l,OOO which simply means nothing. There are now several Senators and Congressmen before the Courts I charged with land frauds. These men have fenced in land called their own thousands upon thousands of I acres. The manner of doing it, as exposed in the ! Courts, makes it clear that the department which has ' had charge of the public lands has been subject to the most corrupt influences, and that hundreds of million- aire land-owners are neither more nor less than thieves. , The Chauncey Depew to whom I have referred above is a member of the Yale University Corporation. The demand is now made by the Press that he resigns from , the Yale Corporation, the University governing body. I The direct charge is that the Depew Improvement Co. obtained 250,000d015. from the Equitable Life Assur- ance Society,which resulted in a loss to the Society of , 200,000d015., which is now in the hands of a receiver. ' A train of victims stretching behind it tells the story of a gigantic real estate steal conducted on the prin- ciples of high finance. This is a dreary and a weary tale, and I am afraid must be somewhat tiresome to my readers. The worst of it is the tendency of all this corruption is to so debase the whole of the streams of social life, that no man, no family, is safe from its consequences. Food adulteration is now engaging the attention of the Government, and the revelations are simply frightful. So it goes on everything and anything to make money, no matter who suffers. The Govern- ment collects statistics of the crops, valuable and im- portant to farmers. These statistics are very valuable also to the speculators in Wall Street that are per- mitted by our stupid laws to gamble in the necessities , of life. One of the Government statisticians, named Holmes, has been caught giving cotton •

statistics in advance to the Cotton Exchange gamblers, getting his part of the money. Barely two weeks ago a U.S. Senator, addressing an audience of Yale alumni, said :—" Bring on your tainted money; we will purify it with the Yale spirit and consecrate it to the blessed mission of educating noble men to uphold the institutions of our glorious country, and, with wisdom, patriotism and liberal con- I servatism, keep her in the front ranks of the nations of the world.' One of his audience a very wealthy man is president of the Prudential Life, and involved in the stock-jobbing operations of the inside ring of the equitable looters. I might quote some very strange utterances of Rockefeller as to his position in the religious world, and the relationship of wealth to religion but I forbear. You may send your Mr. Dawson to convert the masses, but until the govern- ing and employing party learn their duty its all in vain. What it is all dung for Socialism is another story, but to me it seems the most interesting. H. WHITHAM. San Francisco.

The Examiner, Saturday, August 12, 1905.

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Skelton—Hampson.

The marriage of Mr. William Skelton, of Sheffield, eldest son of Mr. John Skelton, of Boundary House, Stretton, and Miss Mary Elizabeth Hampson, the second daughter of the late Josiah Elampson, of Glas- gow was solemonized at St. Paul's Church. on Sunday. The Rev. R. H. Bowen performed the ceremony. The bride,who was given away by her brother-in-law, Mr. Harry Magee, of Macclesfield, wore a dress of blue taffeta trimmed with white lace and silk, also a white hat trimmed with white chiffon. She carried a white shower bouquet, the gift of the bridegroom. Her bridesmaid, Miss May Osborne, wore grey dress trimmed with white lace and silk, and carried a shower bouquet of yellow marguerites, the gift of the bride- groom. Mr.E. H. Minards acted as best man. After the ceremony a reception was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe, and daring the afternoon the happy couple left for Matlock, where the honeymoon will be spent. Numerous presents were received.

Steel---Gittins.

There was a pretty wedding at St. Michael's Church Abergele, on Wednesday afternoon, the contracting parties being Miss Sarah A. Gittins, third daughter of ,the late Mr. Edward W. Gitttins who for a number of years prior to his death carried on business in War- rington as a stone merchant, and Mr. Thomas S. Steel, solicitor, Sunnyside, Marsh House-lane, Warrington. The *vent mused considerable interest and a large congregation assembled. The Rev. Jones, vicar of Abergele, officiated. The bride was charmingly attired in a dress of biscuit coloured voile trimmed with lace and silk. She wore a toque of cream coloured chiffon and carried a lovely bouquet of white roses, lilies of the valley, stephanotis, annunciation flowers and white heather. The bride- groom's gift to the bride was a gold watch. She was attended by two bridesmaids—Mss Emily Gittins (sister of the bride) and Miss Jessie Steel (sister of the bridegroom). Their dresses were of pink flowered yoil with sashes of pink ribbon, and they wore biscuit coloured hats trimmed with pink roses. They carried beautiful bouquets of pink roses, and their ornaments were gold bangles, the gifts of the bridegroom. The bridegroom's mother was gowned in black voile trimmed with &pule cream lace and carried a bouquet of red carnations. Mr. George H. W. Steel (brother of the bridegroom) discharged the duties of best man.At the conclusion of the ceremony the wedding breakfast was served at the School House, a number of relatives and friends being invited. Daring the afternoon several congratulatory telegrams were received. Later the happy couple left for the Western Highlands of Scot land where the honeymoon is being spent. SANKEY STREET IMPROVEMENTS.

Mr. T. }links' New Premises. The people of Warrington have observed with con- siderable interest the progress which has recently been made in the widening of Sankey street. A big taskt confronts the Corporation before the street can be im- proved to the extent which its importance demands, but sufficient has already been done to show that if a complete scheme of widening is c trried through Sankey street will in years to come be a very handsome thoroughfare. For some weeks workmen have been engaged in the demolition of the old premises of Mr. T.Birtles,photographer,Legh House and the work is now almost completed. The result is that Sankey-street at this particular point has a width of about sixty feet—which, of course, is quite ample for the volume of traffic. It is the intention, we under- stand, to continue this widening to the Town Hall drive. This will necessitate the inclusion in the street of part of the gardens fronting the Education Office and the house formerly occupied hyi, the late Miss Gan-en, and the Corporation offices at Bank House will also have to be pulled down. It is as yet too early to speak of the widening of the street in the Market-gate direction, which must obviously be a very slow procedure as the acquire- ment of property will involve great expenditure. Now that Mr. T. Birtles' old shop and studio have been demolished, the public have an opportunity of I seeing the handsome new premises which have been built. They will be opened for business next week, and we think our readers will agree that they are a I credit to the town. The new building is of the moat modern description, and is admirably adapted for the purposes of photography. On entering from Sankey-street the visitor finds hunself in a commodious • and well-appointed reception room and picture gallery —nu entirely new feature for Warrington—in which will be hung a fine selection of Mr. Birtles' recent works. At the end of this room is the entrance to the studio which in size can certainly be regarded as one of the ' finest in the country. Adjoining it are dressing-rooms ' fitted with every convenience. Underneath the studio are the dark rooms, enlarging and printing roams, all of the most up-to-date character. The open space at the rear of the buildings will be specially arranged for of groups,g horses, and outdoor advantage, it may be i pthicetuprehsotgogenraeprahlilng y. One stated, s that practically the whole of the new premises are on the ground floor. The excellence of Mr. Birtles' work is, of course, well known to War- ringtonians,his newest productions deserving of special attsntion being the permanent ivory miniatures which are most pleasing and artistic.

FOOTBALL CLUB SPORTS.

SUCCESSFUL BANK HOLIDAY ATHLETIC FESTIVAL

For twenty years the athletic sports promoted by the Warrington Football Club have been the great August Bank Holiday attraction at Warrington, and a splendid attendance at Monday's festival was proof that the popularity of the sports is well maintained. A crowd of about 7,000 lined the Wilderspool enclo- sure and the officials of the club had every reason to congratulate themselves upon gaining that liberal public patronage which is the first essential to a successful meeting. While the weather was far from being all that one could desire, it was gratifying that the conditions did not interfere' to any great extent with the carrying out of the programme. The after- noon opened threateningly, but soon after the sports began, there was a good spell of bright sunshine. Later the clouds gathered, and rain fell almost continuously to tho close of the festival. Most of the spectators, however, remained to see the whole of the races. The committee had been fortunate in

-- securing a large entry, but the " talent" was perhaps not quite up to the usual standard. A great dis- appointment was in store for the spectators, as H. A Hyman, of the University- of Pennsylvartia, the 220 yards amateur champion of the world, who had promised to compete, telegraphed on Monday that he had wrenched his knee in a race at Glasgow on Satur- day, and it was therefore impossible for him to appear. There were, however, many exciting contests, and local athletes were well to the fore.

WARRINGTON WORKHOUSE OVERCROWDING.

BUILDING SCHEME TO BE AGAIN BROUGHT FORWARD. Discussion at the Board Meeting.

The question of the overcrowding of the Warring- ton Workhouse is twain occupying the attention of the Warrington Board of Guardians and at the next meet- ing a proposal to re-adopt the building scheme which I was in March deferred for twelve months will be brought forward. A preliminary step to enable this II to be done was taken at the meeting of the Board on I Wednesday when the members agreed to the rescind- I ing of the resolution of March Bth, deferring the con- sideration of the erection of an infirm block for twelve months. The whole question will therefore he re- discussed at the next meeting. It will be remembered that at the last Board meeting it was resolved that "the existing Workhouse accom- modation being unsatisfactory, steps be taken forthwith for the provision of proper andsufficient accommodation for the indoor poor, especially the sick and aged." A Special Committee was appointed to deal with the matter and the Chairman received a letter from Messrs. William and Segar Owen,architects explaining with regard to Mr. Hickman's proposal to utilize the old infirmary that the estimate of £1,500 to .21,650 was for the work which the Local Government Board architect would require to make the buildings suitable for what was proposed should be their use. To merely carry out the alterations which Mr. Hickman proposed, would not cost more than half that amount, but the bare altera- tions would not meet with the Local Government Board's approval. With regard to the total estimate for Mr. Hickman's scheme of £3,000 to £3,500 it would be understood that by the alterations to the Infirmary, all workshops were absorbed, and it would render necessary the building of new workshops of considerable area. The special committee unanimously recommended "that the resolution arrived at by the Board on March Bth last, deferring the consideration of the erection of an infirm block at the Workhouse for twelve months be resciaded."

At the Board meeting the CLERK (Mr. J. C. Sutton) in answer to Mr. T. H. Ashcroft, stated that the Special Committee consisted of the chairman (Mr. J. Billinge) and vice-chairman (Mr. W. Hickman) of the Board, the chairman (Mr. R. Forshaw) and vice-chairman (Mr. G. Thompson) of the House Committee,and Mr. S. Davies. The CHAIRMAN (Mr. .J. Billinge) moved the adoption of the special committee's resolution. He expressed regret thatan important business engagement prevented Mr. Hickman's attendance at the special committee meeting but said he understood that would not occur again, as Mr. Hickman had retired from business. The Board was quite familiar with all the details of the I scheme, He did not expect any opposition to the com- mittee's recommendation, because he kno v the members of the Board would not wish to inflict any hardship on the sick, aged and infirm in their institutions. One felt thankful that the Warrington Union was in such Ifavourable circumstances that an expenditure of £8,500 would meet present requirements. In neighbour- ing unions schemes were being brought forward to cost as much as £75,000 and £160,000 respectively. A farthing on the assessable value of the Union would meet all the requirements for Warrington. When the new buildings were in use they would prob- ably be able to save a fifth of that farthing by better administration which would result, and also by apply- ing the "test" of the institution. Mr. FORSHA.W seconded.

Mr. lircKm AN said that in view of the meagre attend- ance at that meeting owing to the holidays, he would move an amendment that the matter be deferred until the meeting on the 23rd inst. - . .

Mr. Asd-cnorr saconded. He did not think the recommendation should be brought forward, in view of the fact that the Board had previously decided to defer the question for twelve months. Some members, how- ever, were determined to push the scheme on, although the new building was not reauired. The hospital was plenty large enough, and as for the accommodation in the House, the number of inmates on Friday was only 4H. There was a certain individual at the back of the matter who was pushing the scheme on. The public of Warrington felt warmly on the question, and he (Mr. Ashcroft) hoped the members who were moving for all the expenditure and extravagance would be pushed off the Board when they had to face the electors. Mr. HOUGHTON : Th this in order? I don't want to have to stop hare all morning. Mr. ASiienorT : I have been over the buildings and I can prove that we do not require the extension. Continuing he contended that there were individuals who from selfish motives were trying to pass the scheme. If a strong committee were formed to go into the matter thoroughly the scheme would never go on, because they would find there was no necessity for it. I Interrupted by a member Mr. ASHCROFT exclaimed "I wish some of you would go back and never come here any more."

Miss .i3ROADBBNT said she thought Mr. Jenner-Fust made it very clear that the extension was necessary,and the Board might take his word as being an expert on the subject. It was very tedious to hear the same argu- ments brought up again and again, and it was a great waste of time.

Mr. ASHCROFT : Can I rise again ? I will answer Mr. Jenner Fust (" Oh, oh "and laughter). Mr. FORSHAW : You might take his word. Mr. ASHCROFT : I know more about this place than you do. The REV. Da. MEitoEn did not see that there was any need for a fight over the resolution, as it would leave 1 the Board absolutely free afterwards. It was a very mild resolution and would leave the Board in exactly the same position as before. With all due respect to the Special Committee, however, the members of the Board would require reasons to induce them to change their votes. He voted against the scheme when it came up before, and he should vote against it again unless he knew there were some fresh facts to cause him to change his opinion. Mr. Fortsßaw thought the amendment was quite superfluous. Mr. RICKMAN : lam simply looking at the meagre attendance at the meetting. Mr. FORSHA w, continuing, said there was a misappre- hension about the question. It was not a question of the housing capacity of the whole of the premises but of the accommodation in the hospital. It was absolutely essential that the hospital wards should only be used for the classes of cases for which they were specially intended. Dr. Mercer asked if there were any new facts. The reason the matter had been again brought forward was that Dr. Braids in his report on June 30th, stated that the hospital generally had be.m overcrowded for the past six months. If the place was overcrowded now, what would it be when winter came The care of the sick poor was the first duty of a Guardian. How could they be taking care of those people when they had, as had been the case during the past week,a mad patient put amongst the inmates in the hospital because there was northere else to accommo- date him. He was screaming and yelling all night and oven the master could hear him in the hall. What, therefore, mudt the particular ward have been like? Owing to the lack of proper accommodation they had had to use the straight jacket a great deal too much, in fact he (Mr. Forshavv) thought it was improper to use it like they had done. H.M. Inspector had commented on the matter and a special committee was appointed to go into the qnestion of the padded room which was in disrepair. He did not think Mr. Hickman's scheme to meat the congestion by altering the old hospital would help them very much. There were in that hospital 30 beds and 30 were now being used. During the months of January, February and March there were always over 70 inmates of the old hospital, so that there was little margin. The old hospital was already being used to its fullest extent. It was evident from Dr. Braide's report that there was very serious congestion in the hospital. Those who were opposed to the erection of a new block should come prepared with an alternative scheme. Anything which they proposed would be carefully gone into. If they were not prepared with a constructive policy, he would ask : Where was the logic of their position ? Would it not be far better to leave the matter to those who were constructive and who wished to bring it to a satisfac- tory conclusion ? Mr. HICKMAN said that in view of the fact that the question would he thrashed out at the next meeting he would withdraw his amendment. The opposition would be prepared with a constructive policy. The other side had effectively killed one alternative scheme, hut another would be brought forward and they would have an opportunity to kill that, if they could. Mr. Asficitorr said he could not understand why the amendment should be withdrawn. The scheme was deferred for twelve months and it ought to be left alone until the expiration of that period. On a vote being taken the committee's recommenda- tion rescinding the Board's original resolution was confirmed, ten voting for it and no one against. In reply to Mr. MATHER, the CLERK said he expected to have sufficient balance to be able to fix a rate of 7d. 'for the next half year. Mr. BOLTON asked if some influential ratepayers could be allowed to visit the institution to judge for them- selves as to the accommodation. He felt very strongly on the question. The CHAIRMAN : I think the Warrington people have confidence in their Guardian; and think they can leave their interests with them. Mr. BOLTON: Imy they have not on this question. Mr. ASHCROFT : Would Mr. Bolton be allowed to bring a large ratepayer bore and show him round ? The CHAIRMAN : He can talk the matter over with the Clerk some day. The CLERK: lam afraid the CLERK has no power in the matter?

Mr. 14 onsirx : I should like to see some large ratepayers here. The Rev. Dr. MERCER: The Chairman should invite them as his guests. The matter then dropped. £8,430 EXPENDITURE PROPOSED. At a meeting of the Board on Wednesday, August 23rd, the following resolution will be submitted That the Guardians hereby cement : —(1) To the erection at the Workhouse of the Union of buildings

for the accommodation of the infirm in accordance with plans to which the Local Government Board have affixed their seal, subject nevertheless to such modifica- tions: thereof as that Board may hereafter direct. (2) To the provision of furniture for the said buildings. (3) To the expense for the above purposes of a sum not exceeding £8,430. (4) To the borrowing of the said sum,lof £8,430 or such part thereof as may be necessary for the purposes aforesaid. It is proposed that of the sum borrowed £7,780 be repaid within a period not exceeding 30 years and the sum of £550 within a period not exceeding 15 years.

The members of the Runcorn Board of Guardians spent some considerable time at their meeting on Tues- day discussing a scheme for extensive alterations to the Workhouse at Dutton and for the erection of new buildings. Mr. A. R. Norman preaided,nd a there were present Messrs. C. Roylance, J. Bolshaw, H. Eyes, G. Egerton Warburton, J. Stokes, A. Broom, T. Baxter, C. E. Linaker, J. H. DaViPfl, 3. Smith, T. Wright, T. Guest. S. Woodward, G. Pilling, J. Fryer. J. Spruce, S. Taylor, P. Collins, J. Donglas, J. W. Temperley, .J. Ratter, .1. Hallows, ,T. Whitley, W. Weir, T. Antwis. Admiral Jones Pat ry and Messrs. W. E. Hough (assistant clerk), Riley, De Prez, and Pearson (relieving officer). PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE WORKHOUSE. Messrs. Wm.. and Seger Owerearchitects, IVarrington, wrote enclosing plans of the Workhouse antlethe Infir n- ary buildings for consideration in Connection with the promised alterations and additions to the Workhouse and also plans of the alterations and additions. They stated that the Guardians considered the old fever hospital and the late married couple's 'quitters were in such a ruinous condition and so obsolete in arrange- merit that it would be wisest to take there doyen using the ground as a kitchen garden and drying ground. The morteary should he removed to another position when the position of the nurses home and maternity ward ; were fixed. They considered the decision to take down ! the old buildings a wise one. They showed on the plan the position in which the nurses home should stand and the accommodation it should provide. Accommodation for six nurses was recommended. It as thought that there should be two committee rooms and a relieving officers' room. They showed in plan how the girls' school on the ground floor could b i e made into a large committee room and the present com- mittee room divided so as to form a small committee room and a room for the relieving officers. The latter could also be used as the masters office, the present office to be taken into the stores department as a sewing room. The greatest pressure on the aocommo- dation of the House was in the men's dormitory. The boys dormitories were never fully occupied. A satis- factory alteration could be made by tatting from the letter sntficient space for the accommodation of another 20 men and a smaller part from the men's dormitory to pro- vide accommodation for flve boys a sittiee room and bed- room for the industrial trainer who at present had only one room. They further showed how a new maternity ward could be added to the women's wing. A number of minor alterations were recommended. The total , estimated cost was £2,245. Mr. Woodward said the committee which had been appointed had gone carefully into the matter and had reduced the estimated cost tAL2,018.18. They especially ' considered the suggestion of IL M. Inspector.l , Mr. Haney, with reference to the foul ward, . and he was bound to say they did not agree with the suggestion. Mr. Dancy suggested the cutting off of two ventilating lobbies between the foul ward and the main building, The committee were how- ever of opinion that it was not in the interest of the health and comfort of the departments affected to those ventilating lobbies should be done away with, and suggested their being re-instated. That he believed would mean som a little increase in the cost of the alteration. He understood Mr. Dency had been strongly condemning the Board or its committees for spending too much money, but he would like to point out that Mr. Dancy's suggestions with regard to the alterations had largely increased the cost of the work. He moved the adoption of tke report of the committee. Mr. Pilling seconded. Mr. Spruce said the cost of the propesed nurses home —£l,ooo—seemed to be very high. The committee con- tetnplated an expenditure of between £aoo and £7OO. Mr. Norman said that with regard to the nurser. home ' they were looking ahead, but he did not think they were wise in looking quite so far in the future. He hoped the number of inmates would not go on increas- ing to such an 'extent that they would have to double their staff. Mr. S. Woodward said they must remember that the accommodation for the nurses was altogether inefficient. If they had to make increased accommodation he did rot think it wise for them to restrict themselves to the requirements of the moment. He could assure the Board he was not in favour of spending money if he could avoid it. The Chairman : If the children are boarded out will it make any difference? Mr. Woodward : Not in this particular case. The Chairman : everything points to the fact that sooner or later the children will have to be taken from the House. Mr. Spruce moved that the report referring to the nurses home be referred back for he purposes of a reduction being made. In his judgment £7OO should be ample, Mr. Woodward: Mr. Spruce is a member of the com- mittee, and so far as I understand he agreed with the report. Mr. Spruce: I was not at the last meeting of the mittee. At a former meeting it was felt that £7OO would be ample. Mr. Seger Owen explained that buildings cost more at Dutton on account of the isolated situation. The actual cost would probably be less than the esti- mate. He did not think the accommodation was any- thing like too much.' Mr. Baxter said the question was as to whether it was necessary to e'-dye so much accommodation. He regarded £l,OOO as too high a figure. Mr. Hallows did not see that it Was necessary to spend £l,OOO on a building to accommodate four purees and a servant. He thought they should be able to build a suitable house for R6OO or £7OO. Admiral Jones Parry said the question of the accom- modation of the nurses. had been constantly before the Board. The inefficient state of the present accommoda- tion was one of the reasons why they could never get nurses to stay. They had spent enough money on advertisements for nurses to pay for the building. Mr. Owen said the plans were of the plainest possible description and allowed for two rooms IS feet by 15 feet, a kitchen 12 feet by 10 feet and scullery Su:. The kitchen accommodation was sufficient for a small enlargement of the staff but if the staff wns increased to ten nurses it would have to be increased. The Chairman: Would it not be possible to put up a smaller building which could easily be extended? Mr. Owen: This building is being put up with that idea. The Chairman paid he thought it building to accom- modate the present staffwvould be sufficient. Mr. Linaker said it appeared to him that the only exception to the plans seemed to be tbe nurses home, and he believed the home could be erected according to the plans submitted for a figure approaehing that men- tioned by Mr. Spruce. He thought the committee had acted quite wisely in its decision. Mr. Antwis : Is this alteration being undertaken with the idea of keeping the nurses better than we have done in the past ? Mr. Norman : That is one of the objects. The chief trouble seems to be on account of the isolatei position of the workhouse. Mr. Antwis : It strikes me if we want to keep the nurses we will have to go nearer Frodsham or War- rington. The Chairman t The Clerk has suggested that we should lay the plans before the Local Government Board as if there was any extravagance they would keep it down. Ido not think they would. Mr. Roylancs pointed out that from time-to time' there had been serious complaints about the accom- modation for the nurses being inseicient. ST. HELENS COLLIERY FATALITY. THE EXAMINER. AUGUST 12th 1905.

Visitors to Bank Park have often regretted their in- ability to inspect the collections housed in the con- servatories except from the outside. They will there- fore be pleased to learn that the Corporation are throwing one of the hot houses,near the Drill Hall,open for the public to view the excellent show of gloxinias. We would again remind Liberals of the importance of seeing that they are duly registered on the list of voters. The burgess list can be seen on the door of any place of worship, and claims must be handed in not later than August 20th. Mr. J. Howes, junior,the Liberal agent, will be pleased to give any assistance - in the matter. Mr. .1. T. Foard, revising barrister, will hold the annual revision court at the Town Hall on September Bth, 9th, and 11th. *s* The Special Committee anpointed to deal with the question of Warrington Bridge widening have not delayed taking action. A deputation has been selec- ted to wait upon Admiral Nares, with the object of inducing him to reconsider his refusal to sanction the widening of the bridge by means of cantilevers on the ground that it would impede navigation. It is to be hoped that they will succeed in their negotiations, and thus avoid the much more expensive alternative of erecting a new bridge.
